# Current News
Primary sources only for the F-1 to OPT to STEM OPT to H-1B track, rebuilt on 2026-10-23 with Firecrawl and checked against court dockets, the Federal Register, and agency pages. This vault uses it as the place to re-check a rule, a date, or a dollar figure before [[VISA Plan]] or [[Junior Year Internship Decision]] relies on it. Read it first when anything in those notes cites a deadline, and re-verify anything older than a few weeks.
> [!WARNING]
> Everything here was true on 2026-10-23 and most of it is moving. Law firm blogs, news sites, and social posts were used to find documents, never as the source of a claim, except where a row says "secondary". The UMN section is deliberately left as it was found and is due for a rewrite.
> Each resource below carries one ==highlighted== line: the single fact it gave that the others did not.
## Where Things Stand
==ICE did not change the CPT regulation. It changed how DSOs must read it through two broadcast messages, and a court has not yet touched that.==
- *Blocked by a court:* the duration of status rule (since Sept 14) and the $100,000 H-1B payment (since June 8, appeals pending).
- *In force and untested:* the CPT guidance. The first court date is Nov 6.
- *Proposed, not law:* the $70,000 and $30,000 OPT fees, the $103,265 H-1B petition fee, the DOL wage rule.
- *Final and new in the last 30 days:* the end of automatic EAD extensions (Oct 14).
## How Real Are The Dates
| Date | Event | Verified from | Confidence | What could move it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2026-10-28 | Government response due on CPT injunction motion | CPT docket, order of Oct 19 | High | Another extension request |
| 2026-11-06, 3:00 PM | CPT injunction hearing, Courtroom 3, Judge Kobick | CPT docket, notice of Oct 13 | High for the hearing, none for a ruling date | Continuance; ruling could come the same day or later |
| 2026-11-09 | OPT fee comments close | Federal Register, ICE BCM 2610-02, DHS release | High (three agreeing primary sources) | Rare, but DHS can reopen or extend |
| 2026-11-09 | Government brief due, First Circuit duration of status appeal | First Circuit docket 26-2112, Oct 19 entry | High | Expedited schedule can shift |
| 2026-11-10 | 60-day grace period comments close | Federal Register 91 FR 57807 | High | Same |
| 2026-11-13 | Comments close on removing deference to prior approvals | Federal Register 91 FR 65119 | High | Same |
| 2026-12-07 | OPT fee paperwork comments close | Federal Register 91 FR 64566 | High | Same |
| 2027-09-21 | Proclamation 11069 restriction ends | Proclamation text, 91 FR 60497 | High as written, low as a forecast | Already renewed once, a second renewal is plausible but unannounced |
| Unknown | Final OPT fee rule | Nothing set | None | Effective 60 days after publication of a final rule |
| Unknown | Ruling on CPT motion; First Circuit and Ninth Circuit H-1B rulings | Dockets show no ruling dates | None | Court discretion |
The Nov 7 comment date seen in some blogs was a pre-publication estimate and is wrong. The published notice says Nov 9.
## CPT Guidance
1. **BCM 2608-01, Aug 12, 2026** ([ICE PDF](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm260801.pdf))
	*Weight:* primary. Read from the PDF itself.
	==DSOs should authorize CPT "only if its absence would make the attainment of a degree an impossibility and if the work associated with CPT is required for all students pursuing that degree."== It also says SEVP "recognizes that the school, not the U.S. government, defines what is and is not required to complete a degree," which is the one opening it leaves schools.
2. **BCM 2608-02, Aug 24, 2026** ([ICE PDF](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm_260802.pdf))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==Question 3 closes the elective route: an optional elective course makes the CPT optional, so it fails.== Question 6 requires a cooperative agreement between the employer and the school. Question 7 announces a confidential SEVP tipline for DSOs who feel pressured by their own leadership.
3. **ICE SEVIS "What's New" index** ([ice.gov](https://www.ice.gov/sevis/whats-new))
	*Weight:* primary, the official list of broadcast messages.
	==Bookmark this to check for a new CPT message before trusting any blog.== It lists the Oct 8 OPT fee message and the July 17 duration of status notice.
4. **8 CFR 214.2(f)(10), version dated July 1, 2026** ([eCFR](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2))
	*Weight:* primary law.
	==The regulation's CPT sentence never says "required for all students": it says "an integral part of an established curriculum" and lists "required internship or practicum."== Use the July 1 version, not "current." The current eCFR view already prints the postponed duration of status wording (30 days) where the operative rule says 60.
## Lawsuit Over CPT
1. **Docket, AAU v. DHS, No. 1:26-cv-14520** ([CourtListener](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/74919449/association-of-american-universities-v-us-department-of-homeland/))
	*Weight:* primary, RECAP copy of PACER, 24 entries through Oct 23.
	==Judge Julia E. Kobick set the hearing for Nov 6 at 3:00 PM and gave the government until Oct 28 to respond.== No ruling, no temporary restraining order, nothing that pauses the guidance.
2. **Complaint** ([PDF hosted by NAFSA](https://www.nafsa.org/sites/default/files/media/document/CPT%20Complaint%20AAU%20v.%20DHS%20.pdf))
	*Weight:* party filing, read in full.
	==Paragraph 5 states the old rule: CPT was allowed when it earned academic credit, or, when it earned none, when it was mandatory.== The Broadcasts move the "mandatory" test onto credit-bearing training too. Claims are Administrative Procedure Act counts: arbitrary and capricious, retroactive, contrary to law, no notice-and-comment. Paragraph 2 lists computer science among the affected fields.
3. **Memorandum supporting the injunction** ([AAU PDF](https://www.aau.edu/uploads/files/general/Memo-in-Support-of-PI-and-Stay-AAU-v-DHS.pdf))
	*Weight:* party filing, 56 pages, skimmed for the case number and dates, not read line by line.
	==Document 9-1, filed Oct 5, is where the harm evidence sits (mid-semester halts and cancelled placements).== Read it if the hearing outcome matters for a specific plan.
4. **NAFSA litigation page** ([nafsa.org](https://www.nafsa.org/legal-defense-CPT))
	*Weight:* plaintiff, last updated Oct 8.
	==Lists the four plaintiffs and counsel (Jenner & Block) and links the filings.== It gives the hearing date but not the time or judge, so use the docket for those.
## OPT Fee Proposal
1. **Federal Register, "Optional Practical Training Fees," 91 FR 64566** ([notice](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees))
	*Weight:* primary, read in full. Docket ICEB-2026-0100, RIN 1653-AB01.
	==The $70,000 applies to the first OPT recommendation of any type, so pre-completion OPT would spend it, and a later post-completion OPT would cost $30,000.== The notice says DHS "acknowledges that the schools may pass the financial obligation" to F-1 students or employers, and the fee would apply to recommendations dated on or after 60 days after a final rule.
2. **ICE BCM 2610-02, Oct 8** ([ICE PDF](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm2610-02.pdf))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==It describes the $30,000 as a charge for "the second year of practical training," which is looser than the rule text, so the Federal Register wording governs.== It confirms Nov 9 and Dec 7.
3. **DHS press release, Oct 7** ([dhs.gov](https://www.dhs.gov/news/2026/10/07/dhs-proposes-fees-foreign-student-work-program-protect-american-jobs))
	*Weight:* primary, last updated Oct 20.
	==The stated purpose is to make schools "exercise greater oversight and selectivity," meaning the fee is a lever on schools, not students.==
4. **WR Immigration, "Fifteen Things to Know"** ([wolfsdorf.com](https://wolfsdorf.com/fifteen-things-to-know-about-dhss-proposed-opt-fees/))
	*Weight:* secondary, an immigration law firm.
	==DHS's own estimate of 194,000 OPT and 95,000 STEM OPT participants in 2024, and its admission that some schools may decline to fund OPT, are the numbers to quote.== Cross-checked: the pass-through language is in the notice. I did not check the participant counts against the notice.
## Duration Of Status
1. **Federal Register final rule, 91 FR 44976, July 17, 2026** ([notice](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/07/17/2026-14439/establishing-a-fixed-time-period-of-admission-and-an-extension-of-stay-procedure-for-nonimmigrant))
	*Weight:* primary. Docket ICEB-2025-0001, RIN 1653-AA95.
	==Admission would be fixed to the I-20 end date, capped at four years, with an extension of stay needed for OPT.== Effective date Sept 15, 2026, now postponed.
2. **District docket, No. 1:26-cv-13799** ([CourtListener](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/74661796/presidents-alliance-on-higher-education-and-immigration-v-united-states/))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==Judge Saylor's Sept 14 order is relief under Administrative Procedure Act section 705, which postpones the effective date. The docket titles it a preliminary injunction.== Vacatur and summary judgment were denied without prejudice.
3. **First Circuit appeal, No. 26-2112** ([CourtListener](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/74908049/presidents-alliance-on-higher-educ-and-immigr-v-dhs/))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==The government asked only to expedite the appeal. No stay of the Sept 14 order was requested in the entries I saw.== Its brief is due Nov 9.
4. **Presidents' Alliance litigation tracker** ([page](https://www.presidentsalliance.org/duration-of-status-litigation/))
	*Weight:* plaintiff, kept current.
	==Gives the district schedule: administrative record Oct 23, supplemental briefs Oct 26, replies Nov 2.==
## Graduating In 2028: The OPT Path
This assumes a May 2028 program end date on the I-20. Confirm the exact date with the DSO, because every window below counts from it.
1. **USCIS, OPT for F-1 Students** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/students-and-exchange-visitors/[REDACTED]))
	*Weight:* primary, last updated Nov 25, 2024, so it predates the duration of status rule.
	==Pre-completion OPT is deducted from post-completion at a rate that depends on hours: one year part-time costs 6 months, one year full-time costs the whole 12.== File with USCIS up to 90 days before and no later than 60 days after the program end date, and within 30 days of the DSO recommendation. A timely STEM OPT filing gets an automatic 180-day extension.
2. **USCIS, Form I-765 page** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/i-765))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==The (c)(3)(B) row repeats the 90-days-before, 60-days-after window, confirming USCIS still applies the old rule while the new one is postponed.== EAD cards ship within about 2 weeks of approval, allow 30 days before inquiring.
3. **USCIS, Premium Processing** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/forms/all-forms/how-do-i-request-premium-processing))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==Premium processing is open to pre-completion, post-completion, and STEM OPT I-765s, with a 30-business-day guarantee.== The fee is on Form G-1055, which I did not read.
4. **Reddy Neumann Brown, "OPT Delays in 2026"** ([rnlawgroup.com](https://www.rnlawgroup.com/[REDACTED]/))
	*Weight:* secondary, an immigration law firm. The rule it cites is real: 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(A)(3) requires all OPT within 14 months of completion.
	==Because employment starts on the approval date, a late EAD permanently shortens the 12 months.== Its claim that USCIS delays now stretch to months is unverified, since USCIS's processing-times tool blocked my fetch.
5. **8 CFR 214.2(f)(10) to (12), July 1, 2026 version** ([eCFR](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2))
	*Weight:* primary law.
	==STEM OPT requires an E-Verify employer, a signed Form I-983 training plan, and a new I-983 within 10 days of changing employers.== OPT grace after authorization ends is 60 days.
6. **USCIS, cap-gap page** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/temporary-workers/[REDACTED]/[REDACTED]))
	*Weight:* primary, last updated Jan 17, 2025.
	==Cap-gap extends OPT and F-1 status to April 1 of the H-1B fiscal year, covers only cap-subject petitions, and does not apply to cap-exempt employers such as universities.== Unemployment limits sit here too: 90 days on initial OPT, 150 with STEM OPT.
7. **Federal Register final rule, "Removal of Automatic Extensions for Certain EADs," 91 FR 65306** ([notice](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/14/2026-20955/removal-of-automatic-extensions-for-certain-employment-authorization-documents))
	*Weight:* primary, effective Oct 14, 2026.
	==Renewal EADs no longer stay valid while a renewal is pending.== The text does not mention OPT or STEM OPT, and STEM OPT's own 180-day extension is a separate provision (8 CFR 274a.12(b)(6)(iv)), so I read it as unaffected. That is my reading, not a USCIS statement.
## H-1B Path For A 2028 Graduate
1. **USCIS, H-1B Cap Season** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/temporary-workers/[REDACTED]/h-1b-cap-season))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==Wage level IV registrations enter the lottery 4 times, level III 3, level II 2, level I once.== If the degree is not yet awarded, the petition can use a final transcript or a registrar's letter confirming all requirements are met. Filing opens April 1 for a start of Oct 1.
2. **USCIS, H-1B Electronic Registration** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/temporary-workers/[REDACTED]/[REDACTED]))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==FY2027 registration ran March 4 to 19, 2026, cost $215 per registration, and eligible registrations fell from 470,342 (FY2025) to 343,981 (FY2026).== Expect FY2029 to follow the same March pattern, which is a forecast.
3. **USCIS, FY2027 selection completed, Mar 31, 2026** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/newsroom/alerts/[REDACTED]))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==The petition filing window is "at least 90 days," so a May graduate can finish the degree before it closes.== That is my inference, not USCIS's statement.
4. **TRAC, "Fundamental Changes to H-1B Visas"** ([tracreports.org](https://tracreports.org/reports/768/))
	*Weight:* research organization at Syracuse University, built from the DHS rule's own data.
	==Modeled selection odds: about 15.3 percent at wage level I against 61.2 percent at level IV, versus 29.6 percent for everyone under the old random draw.== These are modeled, not actual FY2027 results.
5. **Proclamation 11069, Sept 18, 2026** ([Federal Register](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/09/23/2026-19554/restriction-on-entry-of-certain-nonimmigrant-workers))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==It reports that registrations for beneficiaries with a U.S. master's rose from 45.1 to 66.1 percent of the total in one year, and that job offers at the lowest wage level were about 17.8 percent of selections.== Those are the government's figures and its own justification for the policy.
6. **USCIS, H-1B Specialty Occupations** ([uscis.gov](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/[REDACTED]))
	*Weight:* primary.
	==The $100,000 payment "does not apply" to an in-U.S. change of status that is granted, which covers F-1 to H-1B.== It does apply if the change of status is denied or the person leaves the U.S. before it is decided.
7. **Court dockets for the $100,000 payment**
	*Weight:* primary. [First Circuit No. 26-1699](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/73476893/state-of-california-v-mullin/), [Ninth Circuit No. 26-6667](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/74966923/global-nurse-force-et-al-v-trump-et-al/), [D.C. Circuit No. 25-5473](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/72095497/chamber-of-commerce-of-the-united-states-of-ameri-v-dhs/).
	==Three appeals are pending in three circuits, and the D.C. Circuit has had argument since March 2026 with no decision.== The First Circuit denied reconsideration on Sept 21. Three courts reaching different results is how a split forms.
8. **Federal Register proposals** ([$103,265 fee](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/08/25/2026-17324/[REDACTED]), [DOL wage rule](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/03/27/2026-06017/improving-wage-protections-for-the-temporary-and-permanent-employment-of-certain-foreign-nationals), [no deference to prior approvals](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/14/2026-20941/removing-deference-to-prior-uscis-determinations-of-eligibility), [60-day grace period](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/09/11/2026-18631/[REDACTED]))
	*Weight:* primary, all proposed.
	==The $103,265 fee would apply to all cap-subject petitions including the master's exemption, the new rule would end deference for H-1B extensions, and the grace-period rule lists E, H-1B, L-1, O-1 and TN but not F-1.== None is final. The first two are the ones that change a 2028 graduate's employer cost.
## What UMN Says
Left as originally found. Stale and queued for a rewrite.
1. **ISSS off-campus work page** ([isss.umn.edu](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/off-campus))
	==Updated Sept 3. ISSS is pausing every CPT application and its only exception is an experience "required for their degree."==
2. **ISSS pre-completion OPT page** ([isss.umn.edu](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/opt/pre-completion-opt))
	==Updated Sept 18. Using all 12 months before graduation forfeits STEM OPT.== It does not mention the pause or the fee, and the USCIS page above gives the more precise deduction rule.
3. **ISSS on-campus employment page** ([isss.umn.edu](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/on-campus))
	==Updated Feb 2025. 20 hours a week in session, full-time in breaks, and "on-campus" includes an educationally affiliated off-campus site.==
4. **CSE Co-op international page** ([cse.umn.edu](https://cse.umn.edu/coop/international-students))
	==Carries the same pause banner, and co-op CPT runs through the course CSE 4896.==
5. **ISSS Duration of Status hub** ([isss.umn.edu](https://isss.umn.edu/duration-of-status))
	==Updated Sept 15. Its FAQ still says the rule "goes into effect on September 15," contradicting the hub banner.== Trust the court dockets above.
## Swapped Out
- *eCFR "current" link:* replaced by the dated July 1 version, because the live view already shows postponed text.
- *CU Boulder status page:* secondary and mistypes a year. Replaced by the USCIS H-1B page and three dockets.
- *USCIS alert on the $100,000 payment:* last reviewed June 15, and silent on change of status. Replaced by the H-1B Specialty Occupations page.
- *NAFSA as the only hearing source:* replaced by the docket entry, which adds the time, courtroom, and judge.
- *Day 1 CPT sites, social posts, and student-visa blogs:* found in searches, not used, because they sell services or repeat each other without sources.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS in writing whether any non-required CPT for a CS major is possible during the pause, and whether any CS course carries a required experience 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] Ask ISSS whether remote work for a foreign employer, done while outside the U.S. over the summer, is allowed under the pause; no source I read answers this
- [ ] Check the CPT docket after Nov 6 for an order, and again after Oct 28 for the government's opposition brief
- [ ] Check USCIS's Case Processing Times page for I-765 OPT yourself; it blocked automated access, so the law firm's delay claim is unverified
- [ ] Read the OPT fee notice's transition language and decide whether to file a comment by Nov 9
- [ ] Ask the CS department and ISSS whether UMN would pay, pass on, or decline the OPT fee if it is finalized
- [ ] Verify FY2027 registration counts (211,600 per one law firm) against a USCIS data release; only FY2026 is official so far
- [ ] Re-check the ISSS CPT page; it said it would "be updated soon" on Sept 3 and was unchanged on Oct 23

(3) Impact on Participation in Pre-Completion OPT
    The initial fee of $70,000 applies when an F-1 nonimmigrant 
participates in OPT for the first time, whether it is pre-completion or

Now the duration of status rule, the largest document. I'll read DHS's own Quick Facts and FAQ, then the rule's executive summary and the sections that touch OPT and academic mobility.

The duration of status picture is clear enough for a rich note. Now the H-1B side: the rest of Proclamation 11069 and Executive Order 14431, which I only partly read.

I'm still in the reading phase: the last three H-1B sources (the DOL wage proposal, the weighted-selection rule, and the EAD rule), and then I'll start writing the notes.

I have what I need on the regulations. The last reading step is the existing vault notes that these visa notes should link into naturally.

No name collisions, and the existing link targets resolve. The five existing notes are empty, so I'll read them (a prerequisite for overwriting) and confirm that, then start writing the new notes.

Everything is ready to write. I'll build the notes in dependency order, starting with the F-1 Visa area note.

Two small fixes: a stray blank line before a heading, and the warning should say exactly what I verified (the OPT filing window wording).

The STEM OPT employer terms are verified, and I'll use them in that note. First, a correction to the on-campus paragraph, where my paraphrase of ISSS distorted what its page says.

Now the full capture of the two CPT broadcast messages, built from the primary PDF text I extracted.

Now the CPT lawsuit note, built from the complaint, the memorandum, and the live docket.

Still building. Ten notes are written and verified so far. Now the OPT Fee Proposal, the most detailed one, then the duration of status rule.

Two significant findings. DHS calls the fee a "crucial first step" in a "graduated strategy," and it says CPT may see "additional actions." I'm adding both to the note.

Now the duration of status rule note, which combines the rule text, DHS's own FAQ, and the litigation record.

JHU's page and ISSS both give March 18, 2027 as the OPT filing cutoff. That's probably in the rule text itself, so I'll check it rather than leave it as an unresolved discrepancy.

Two more corrections to propagate: the First Circuit sequence in this note, and the same March 18 date in the EAD note.

Now the cap-gap note, which connects OPT to H-1B.

Before writing the $100,000 payment note, I want the two district-court rulings from their own dockets rather than relying on secondary summaries.

The dockets correct a secondary claim: the Sept 30 *Global Nurse Force* order was a partial **preliminary injunction**, not a vacatur as some summaries said. I'll use the docket's exact language.

Each note aims to replace reading its sources, and the primary documents are quoted where the exact wording matters. `VISA Plan` is the main map. Its sections are Where I Stand, The Track, What Is Moving, and How We Proceed (seven dated steps), plus a status table and the links between notes. The notes fall into five groups:
- **F-1 base:** `F-1 Visa` (the main area note), `F-1 Employment Options`, and `SEVP and DSOs`.
- **CPT:** `CPT Program`, `CPT Broadcast Messages`, and `CPT Lawsuit`.
- **OPT:** `OPT Program`, `Pre-Completion OPT`, `STEM OPT Extension`, `EAD and OPT Processing`, `OPT Fee Proposal`, and `Duration of Status Rule`.
- **H-1B:** `H1B Visa`, `H-1B Lottery`, `H-1B 100K Payment`, `H-1B Rule Proposals`, and `Cap-Gap Extension`.
- **Planning:** `2028 Graduation Timeline`, `Junior Year Internship Decision`, and `Visa Litigation Tracker`.

**Findings that came from reading the sources in full:**
- **Your school and the CPT case:** The University of Minnesota, Twin Cities is on the Association of American Universities' (AAU) member list, and AAU is the lead plaintiff in the CPT case. A win for the plaintiffs would plausibly cover you, but the court hasn't ruled and the scope isn't set.
- **March 18, 2027 is written into the fixed-period rule.** It's the last day to file OPT without an extension of stay. If that rule returns, the date will have passed before your 2028 filing, so you'd probably file an extension application too. I'd flagged it as a discrepancy earlier; it's actually correct.
- **Pre-completion OPT price:** The OPT fee notice itself says pre-completion plus post-completion OPT would total $100,000, against $70,000 for post-completion alone. It also says schools may pass the fee on to students.
- **On-campus work has a limit that's easy to miss.** An off-campus site counts only if it's tied to the curriculum or to contracted *post-graduate* research.
- **Corrections to earlier work:** The Sept 30 *Global Nurse Force* ruling was a partial preliminary injunction, not a vacatur as some summaries said. The "current" eCFR view already shows rules a court has postponed, so I cite the July 1 version.

**Things to know:**
- I used `pdftotext` on three court and ICE PDFs before `CLAUDE.md` gained the "use mineru" rule. The extracted text was clean and quoted accurately, but I'll use mineru from here.
- I didn't append to the session log, because your global rule says not to write files unless asked.
- I could not verify USCIS's live OPT processing time (the site blocks automated access), or the exact scope of the *Global Nurse Force* injunction (I read the docket, not the opinion).
- UMN's own policy pages are still the stale September snapshot, as you said. `VISA Plan` marks a UMN policy note as `(to create)`.
- I used `==highlights==` once per resource in `Current News`, as you asked, which departs from the vault's one-per-section rule.

The first step is the written questions to ISSS (due Oct 30) listed in `Junior Year Internship Decision`.

======== lines 280,360
                                               INTRODUCTION
         In August 2026, on the eve of a new academic year and semester, the Department of
Homeland Security ("DHS") dramatically narrowed the rules that for more than thirty-five years
have enabled international students to engage in experiential learning that is integral to their
studies. The Department did so not by notice-and-comment rulemaking that acknowledged and
explained the drastic revisions, but instead by a pair of "Broadcasts" that denied that any change
had occurred, purporting to "remind" students and school administrators that conduct they had
openly engaged in for more than three decades has in fact been unlawful all along. The impact was
(as intended) dramatic and immediate. International students with experiential opportunities
scheduled for the coming year had their plans upended--canceling pre-arranged internships,
immediately losing access to laboratories necessary to carry out previously planned research, and
withdrawing from other training opportunities midstream. Colleges and universities have had to
reexamine degree programs on the fly. And school officials were threatened with criminal
prosecution for authorizing experiential learning opportunities permitted by the federal
government's longstanding rules. The Administrative Procedure Act ("APA") exists to prevent
such arbitrary, capricious, and unlawful government action.
         Student internships and other forms of experiential learning have long been an essential
component of higher education in the United States. These opportunities--often called "practical
training"--allow students to apply their classroom instruction in real-world professional settings:
a law student interning in a judge's chambers during the semester, obtaining invaluable insight
into the American legal system; an art history major completing a cooperative education program
at a museum, learning what museum operation and curatorial work look like first-hand; a
psychology student shadowing a mental health provider to gain critical hands-on clinical
experience. Learning-by-doing training opportunities like these are deeply enmeshed in
                                                          1
educational curricula across U.S. colleges and universities. And for nearly eight decades,
recognizing the importance of experiential learning, the federal government has invited
international students--like all other students--to participate in practical training opportunities.
         Since 1991, federal regulations have authorized college and university officials to approve
practical training for international students that is "an integral part of an established curriculum."
8 C.F.R. � 214.2(f)(10)(i). In establishing this form of practical training--known as "curricular
practical training," or CPT--the federal government made clear that a training opportunity
qualifies as CPT if either: (1) students earn academic credit for it; or (2) if not taken for credit, the
school requires it for the student's degree. And for thirty-five years, in cables, training materials,
and other guidance, the government repeatedly confirmed that both of these pathways for CPT
were permissible--that "for-credit curricular practical training may be mandatory or elective." Yet
in August, DHS abruptly reversed course and announced that training qualifies as CPT only if it
is mandatory for every student in a degree program--where, in the agency's telling, its absence
"would make the attainment of a degree an impossibility." Training that is "elective" thus no longer
qualifies as CPT, even if the student receives academic credit through a course that is part of a
school's established curriculum.
         In so doing, DHS dramatically narrowed the range of practical training opportunities
available to international students: Institutions can authorize CPT for students such as future public
health experts, art historians, and psychology majors only if their practical training is required of
every single student in the relevant degree program. Because a single degree prepares students for
many different careers, however, such training is often not mandatory for every student. For
example, a psychology major headed for academia may not need or benefit from clinical
internships that other psychologists might seek out. Similarly, a public health student who seeks
                                                          2
to study the spread of infectious diseases may not require the same external opportunities as a
student seeking to work directly with at-risk populations. Institutions of higher education are aware
of, and generally embrace, the broad array of careers that students in any particular degree program
might pursue after graduation--and for that reason, they often do not make practicums,
internships, or other experiential learning programs mandatory for every student obtaining that
degree. The institutions, in other words, give students the flexibility to chart their own academic
path tailored to their interests and career goals, as the regulations have long allowed.
         DHS cavalierly tossed aside this longstanding and sensible approach, and it did so in a
manner that entirely disregards process and maximizes impact. The agency announced its change
to CPT eligibility standards through two unsigned Broadcast Messages (the "Broadcasts") issued
to colleges and universities nationwide. Nowhere did the Broadcasts acknowledge that this policy
is new; nowhere did they reconcile the agency's current position with decades of agency guidance
and institutional understanding, or with thousands of student internships and other experiential
learning that were previously authorized; and nowhere did they address the massive reliance
interests of the students, schools, and school officials affected by the Broadcasts. Remarkably, the
Broadcasts insisted that this policy has always been the rule. Worse still, the Broadcasts are backed
with threats and consequences: Institutions that authorize CPT consistent with the government's
longstanding interpretation risk "withdrawal of SEVP certification"--the credential every school
needs to enroll any F-1 international student. Compl. Ex. A, Doc. No. 1-1, at 1-2 ("Ex. A"). The
individual school officials who administer their F-1 student programs are warned of personal
criminal liability for "providing false information"--that is, for approving CPT based on the rules
that have governed for thirty-five years. Compl. Ex. B, Doc. No. 1-2, at 2 ("Ex. B"). And DHS
appears to have begun requiring students who previously completed CPT to justify their practical
======== lines 650,700
         C. Impacts of the Broadcasts
         The Broadcasts took effect immediately, just as the fall semester of a new academic year
was beginning. In direct response to the Broadcasts, many colleges and universities stopped
authorizing CPT for for-credit elective opportunities; began reviewing curricula against the new
interpretation of CPT; and adjusted their advising practices for international students. See, e.g.,
                                                         13
Columbia Decl. �� 6-7, Doc. No. 8-5 (Columbia "has had to deny authorization to over 120
international students for Fall CPT," and "revisit[ed] their Fall career guidance sessions"); WashU
Decl. � 11, Doc. No. 8-14 ("The Broadcasts have forced WashU academic programs to reevaluate
existing curricula."); Cornell Decl. � 16, Doc. No. 8-6 ("many programs are currently considering
formalizing [practical training] experiences as mandatory degree requirements for all students").
Institutions have also rescinded some CPT authorizations for students with placements scheduled
for the current academic year. E.g., NAFSA Decl. � 7, Doc. No. 8-4 (institutions have "rescinded
previously authorized CPT for fall-term internships"); Grinnell Decl. � 10, Doc. No. 8-9 (at least
thirteen students lost Fall 2026 internships in finance, consulting, and information technology).
DSOs have reported anxiety over performing their job functions, given the now-constant threat of
prosecution that hangs over them. NAFSA Decl. �� 16-19 (DSO with over twenty years'
experience "is considering resigning their position and leaving the field altogether"); Dartmouth
Decl. � 14, Doc. No. 8-7 (threats in Broadcasts have "a particularly chilling effect," as DSOs
"cannot reasonably be expected to risk prosecution"); Vanderbilt Decl. � 10, Doc. No. 8-13 ("The
unprecedented tone of the Broadcasts has raised concern that CPT determinations could subject
individual DSOs ... to additional scrutiny, even where they are seeking to comply in good faith.").
And DHS appears to have begun requiring students who previously completed CPT to justify their
practical training under the Broadcasts' new CPT eligibility standards when applying for other
immigration benefits. NAFSA Decl. � 20.
         Plaintiffs are member organizations that represent both institutions and individual DSOs
who have been directly impacted by the Broadcasts. Plaintiff Association of American Universities
("AAU") represents 69 leading research universities in the United States. AAU Decl. � 6, Doc.
No. 8-1. Plaintiff Presidents' Alliance on Higher Education and Immigration ("PA") represents
                                                         14
close to 600 public and private colleges and universities of all sizes and institutional types, located
in forty-two states, D.C., and Puerto Rico. PA Decl. � 3, Doc. No. 8-2. Plaintiff Association of
Independent Colleges and Universities in Massachusetts ("AICUM") represents 55 private
colleges and universities across the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. AICUM Decl. � 3, Doc.
No. 8-3. And Plaintiff NAFSA: Association of International Educators ("NAFSA") serves the
needs of more than 9,000 members and international educators worldwide--including individual
DSOs--with the vast majority of its members located in the United States. NAFSA Decl. �� 2-3.
                                             LEGAL STANDARD
         To obtain a preliminary injunction, the movant must show that "(1) it is likely to succeed
on the merits; (2) it is likely to suffer irreparable harm in the absence of preliminary relief; (3) the
balance of equities tips in its favor; and (4) an injunction serves the public interest." Whoop, Inc.
v. Serinity Grp., 834 F. Supp. 3d 170, 176-77 (D. Mass. 2026) (citing Winter v. Nat. Res. Def.
Council, Inc., 555 U.S. 7, 20 (2008)). When the opposing parties are government entities or

III. PLAINTIFFS' MEMBERS WILL SUFFER IRREPARABLE HARM ABSENT
         INJUNCTIVE RELIEF.
         Harm is irreparable when it is "not accurately measurable or adequately compensable by
money damages." Ross-Simons of Warwick, Inc. v. Baccarat, Inc., 102 F.3d 12, 19 (1st Cir. 1996).
Here, absent an injunction, Plaintiffs' members face the prospect of losing their ability to enroll
international students and personal liability for their DSOs, all while sacrificing practical training
opportunities for students, paying unrecoverable compliance costs, and losing competitive
positioning and goodwill. None of those injuries can be remedied by monetary damages. And all
of the harms are imminent: The Broadcasts went into effect immediately, schools have altered
their practices and CPT approvals in response, and DSOs already report fearing prosecution and
being chilled in the exercise of their regular responsibilities. NAFSA Decl. �� 16-19. Only
injunctive relief can preserve the status quo pending resolution on the merits.
                                                         38
         First, as long as the Broadcasts remain in effect, students enrolled at Plaintiffs' member
institutions will lose unique, time-limited opportunities to engage in practical training. "The loss
of a unique" or "evanescent" opportunity is irreparable because its value "may be extremely
difficult to measure, after the fact." Starlight Sugar, Inc. v. Soto, 114 F.3d 330, 332 (1st Cir. 1997).
Courts in this Circuit have applied that principle repeatedly to students' educational and training
opportunities--including opportunities lost when the government disrupts international students'
F-1 status and training authorization. See Ozturk v. Trump, 819 F. Supp. 3d 45, 61 (D. Mass. 2025)
(student "losing out on a unique opportunity" to pursue doctoral training;); Liu v. Noem, 780 F.
Supp. 3d 386, 403 (D.N.H. 2025) (delay and threat to degree completion irreparable where
program timelines and funding windows cannot be recaptured); Monga v. Nat'l Endowment for
the Arts, 323 F. Supp. 3d 75, 93-94 (D. Me. 2018) ("unique, fleeting, one-time opportunity").
         Here, these lost opportunities will pile up, semester by semester. Schools are currently
considering CPT requests for winter break, and processing for the Spring and Summer 2027
semesters is underway. E.g., PA Decl. � 24; FHDA Decl. �� 13-14; MSU Decl. � 9; UIUC Decl.
� 38; Vanderbilt Decl. � 12; Wisconsin Decl. � 38(a). Moreover, CPT authorizations are tied to
specific employers, specific positions, and specific dates. 8 C.F.R. � 214.2(f)(10)(i). An internship
that begins in October, a spring clinical placement, a winter-break rotation--each exists in a
window, and when the window closes the opportunity is gone. E.g., Cornell Decl. � 19 ("Many
internships ... operate on fixed recruiting and start-date schedules, and require students to commit
months in advance."); MSU Decl. � 9 (students in accounting program will not be able to
participate in internships during tax filing season that begins in January without immediate relief).
For students in the final year of their studies--a significant percentage of students in two- and
four-year programs--there is no later semester in which to make up these opportunities. Their
                                                         39
opportunities are thus not merely delayed; they are denied. Each academic cycle that passes under
the Broadcasts converts more students' plans for practical instruction into unrecoverable losses.
         Second, institutions that follow the regulation as written risk the severe sanctions the
Broadcasts threaten explicitly: loss of SEVP certification. A court in this District has credited
evidence that the loss of this certification would "irreparably harm[]" an institution's "ability to
compete with other institutions for the most qualified applicants at home and abroad." President
& Fellows of Harvard Coll. v. U.S. Dep't of Homeland Sec., 788 F. Supp. 3d 182, 209-10 (D.
Mass.) (quoting record), appeal docketed, No. 25-1627 (1st Cir. July 1, 2025). That threat operates
on institutions now, before any enforcement: It is the reason members have stopped authorizing
CPT opportunities that the regulation's text and longstanding agency interpretation permit. E.g.,
UPenn Decl. � 8; Grinnell Decl. � 9. In addition, DSOs, threatened personally with "penalties for
providing false information," Ex. A at 1, are chilled in advising students and processing requests
for CPT. NAFSA Decl. �� 16-19. The Broadcasts' "threat[s] of enforcement and the uncertainty
that stems from [them] is likely sufficient" to establish irreparable harm. New York v. U.S. Dep't
of Justice, 804 F. Supp. 3d 294, 331 (D.R.I. 2025); see also City & Cnty. of San Francisco v.
Trump, 783 F. Supp. 3d 1148, 1186 (N.D. Cal. 2025) (irreparable harm based on "[u]ncertainty
about whether" challenged executive orders would be enforced).
         The harm from threatened enforcement is particularly clear because the exposure is
retrospective as well as prospective. Institutions and DSOs risk sanctions not only related to
authorizations that postdate the Broadcasts; they face potential consequences for authorizations
that complied fully with the framework in effect when they were granted, see supra pp. 27-29--a
hazard that no later judgment can retroactively remove. Immediate injunctive relief is necessary
to redress the present threat of enforcement hanging over individual DSOs and institutions.
                                                         40
         Third, institutions are incurring unrecoverable compliance costs. Since August 12,
Plaintiffs' member institutions have halted authorizations mid-stream, re-reviewed pending CPT
requests against the new standard, begun auditing curricula program by program, revised student-
facing guidance, and fielded a surge of inquiries from students whose plans the Broadcasts
upended. E.g., MSU Decl. � 7.3.2 (student support offices "have seen a substantial increase in
international student advising appointments"); WashU Decl. � 11 (programs "are undertaking
======== lines 1569,1625
V. THE COURT SHOULD STAY AND ENJOIN THE BROADCASTS AND THEIR
         POLICY.
         Plaintiffs request that the Court provide two forms of temporary relief. First, the Court
should stay the Broadcasts under 5 U.S.C. � 705, postponing their effective date pending judicial
review. A stay is warranted and sensible here because the Broadcasts are a single directive
addressed to every SEVP-certified institution through a single system.12 Second, the Court should
12 Trump v. CASA, Inc., 606 U.S. 831 (2025), does not counsel otherwise. CASA addressed
universal injunctions benefiting nonparties, and "[n]othing" in it "resolves the distinct question
whether the Administrative Procedure Act authorizes federal courts to vacate federal agency
action." Id. at 847 n.10. Indeed, even after CASA, district courts may "grant or deny the functional
equivalent of a universal injunction--for example, by . . . preliminarily setting aside or declining
to set aside an agency rule under the APA." Id. at 873 (Kavanaugh, J., concurring).
                                                         44
issue a preliminary injunction barring Defendants from implementing or enforcing the Broadcasts'
interpretation of the CPT regulation as to Plaintiffs, their members, and their member institutions'
employees and students--including any retroactive application to CPT requested, authorized, or
completed before the Broadcasts issued. See, e.g., Doe v. Trump, 157 F.4th at 80 (affirming grant
of preliminary injunction to associational plaintiffs' members). Even if the Broadcasts are stayed,
injunctive relief is necessary to ensure that SEVP does not punish Plaintiffs or their members based
on the same unlawful interpretation of the CPT regulation.
         Under Rule 65(c), no security is warranted. "[T]he First Circuit has recognized an
exception to the ... bond requirement ... in `suits to enforce important federal rights or public
interests."' Westfield High Sch. L.I.F.E. Club v. City of Westfield, 249 F. Supp. 2d 98, 129 (D.
Mass. 2003) (quoting Crowley v. Local No. 82, Furniture & Piano Moving, 679 F.2d 978, 1000
(1st Cir. 1982), rev'd on other grounds, 467 U.S. 526 (1984)). And courts in this District have
applied this exception in suits to enforce federal or public rights against federal agencies and other
governmental entities. See, e.g., id.; Mass. Fair Hous. Ctr. v. U.S. Dep't of Hous. & Urb. Dev.,
--- F. Supp. 3d ---, 2026 WL 2521944, at *12 (D. Mass. Aug. 26, 2026). The exception fits
perfectly here: Defendants face no monetary loss from an order preserving the regime they have
administered for thirty-five years, and Plaintiffs are nonprofit associations suing to enforce the
APA's substantive and procedural guarantees and to enjoin unlawful governmental action.
                                                CONCLUSION
         For the foregoing reasons, the Court should grant Plaintiffs' motion, preliminarily enjoin
Defendants from implementing or enforcing the Broadcasts' interpretation of 8 C.F.R.
� 214.2(f)(10)(i), including retroactively, and stay the Broadcasts under 5 U.S.C. � 705 until
judicial review concludes. A proposed order is submitted herewith.
                                                         45
Dated: October 5, 2026      Respectfully submitted,

III. Background
A. Legal Authority
    The authority to promulgate this rule rests on multiple, 
independent, and mutually reinforcing statutory sources. The Secretary 
of Homeland Security (Secretary) has broad authority under Section 102 
of the Homeland Security Act of 2002 (HSA) (Pub. L. 107-296, 116 Stat. 
2135), 6 U.S.C. 112, and section 103(a)(1) and (3) of the Immigration 
and Nationality Act (INA), 8 U.S.C. 1103(a)(1), (3), to administer and 
enforce the immigration and naturalization laws of the United States, 
to include the issuance of regulations that he deems necessary.\1\ 
Section 214(a) of the INA, 8 U.S.C. 1184(a), authorizes the Secretary 
\2\ to prescribe the time and conditions of admission of any alien as a 
nonimmigrant.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \1\ Although provisions of the INA discussed in this NPRM refer 
to the ``Attorney General,'' such provisions are now to be read as 
referring to the Secretary of Homeland Security by operation of the 
HSA. See 6 U.S.C. 202(3), 251.
    \2\ Although provisions of the INA discussed in this NPRM refer 
to the ``Attorney General,'' such provisions are now to be read as 
referring to the Secretary of Homeland Security by operation of the 
HSA. See 6 U.S.C. 202(3), 251.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    In the INA, Congress established who may be admitted as F 
nonimmigrants. Specifically, section 101(a)(15)(F) of the INA, 8 U.S.C. 
1101(a)(15)(F), established the F visa classification for bona fide 
students who have a residence in a foreign country and wish to enter 
the United States temporarily and solely for the purpose of pursuing a 
full course of study at an academic or accredited language training 
school. Participating schools must be certified by U.S. Immigration and 
Customs Enforcement's (ICE) Student and Exchange Visitor Program 
(SEVP).\3\ The authority to admit foreign students on a temporary basis 
dates back to the Immigration Act of 1924, in which Congress first 
established a student visa category similar to the modern F visa 
classification, referring to ``[a]n immigrant who is a bona fide 
student . . . who seeks to enter the United States solely for the 
purpose of study at an accredited school . . . which shall have agreed 
to report to the Secretary of Labor the termination of attendance of 
each immigrant student. . . .'' \4\ In the decades that followed, 
regulations codified a hands-on educational program for F-1 
nonimmigrants related to their field of study, known as practical 
training.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \3\ See 8 CFR 214.3.
    \4\ Immig. Act of 1924, Public Law 68-139, 4(e), 43 Stat. 153, 
155 (May 26, 1924); accord INA 101(a)(15)(F)(i), 8 U.S.C. 
1101(a)(15)(F)(i).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    The Secretary of DHS has the authority to regulate the time and 
conditions of nonimmigrants' admission to the United States under INA 
214(a), 8 U.S.C. 1184(a).\5\ In the context of F-1 nonimmigrants, 
setting the parameters of practical training has historically been 
acknowledged as a valid exercise of DHS's statutory authority stemming 
under the ``time-and-conditions'' provision in the INA.\6\ The period 
of practical training prescribed by DHS through its regulations is 
considered part of the ``time'' of an F-1 nonimmigrant's admission to 
the United States, and the requirements for the training are part of 
the ``conditions'' of such a status.\7\ Accordingly, DHS is authorized 
to regulate under INA 214(a)(1), 8 U.S.C. 1184(a)(1), the ability for 
F-1 nonimmigrant students to ``engage in limited periods of employment 
for practical training . . . according to the terms set out in the 
[OPT] [r]ule.'' \8\ The regulations governing F-1 nonimmigrants under 
INA 214(a)(1), 8 U.S.C. 1184(a), are reasonably related to the purpose 
of the visas issued under INA 101(a)(15)(F)(i), 8 U.S.C. 
1101(a)(15)(F)(i).\9\ Furthermore, the INA ``need not specifically 
authorize each and every action taken by [DHS], so long as [such] 
action is reasonably related to the duties imposed upon [the 
Secretary].'' \10\ As part of the conditions of admission, DHS is now 
setting a fee to ensure employers, students, and institutions are 
engaging in appropriate activities consistent with admission as an F-1 
nonimmigrant.\11\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \5\ INA 103(a)(1), (3), 8 U.S.C. 1103(a)(1).
    \6\ See Wash. All. of Tech. Workers v. U.S. Dep't of Homeland 
Sec., 50 F.4th 164, 180-82 (D.C. Cir. 2022). See also Section 102 of 
the Homeland Security Act of 2002 (HSA) (Pub. L. 107-296, 116 Stat. 
2135), 6 U.S.C. 112, and section 103(a)(1) and (3) of the INA, 8 
U.S.C. 1103(a)(1), (3).
    \7\ Wash. All. of Tech. Workers, 50 F.4th at 180-82.
    \8\ Id. at 169.
    \9\ Id. at 179.
    \10\ Narenji v. Civiletti, 617 F.2d 745, 747 (D.C. Cir. 1979).
    \11\ Id. at 168 (noting that while INA 101(a)(15)(F)(i), 8 
U.S.C. 1101(a)(15)(F)(i), sets the terms of entry for F-1 
nonimmigrant students, INA 214(a)(1), 8 U.S.C. 1184(a)(1), provides 
DHS additional authority to regulate the ``time'' and ``conditions'' 
of the students' stay where the regulation is reasonably related to 
the terms of the F-1 visa).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    Within DHS, ICE administers SEVP and the Student and Exchange 
Visitor Information System (SEVIS), which collects information related 
to nonimmigrant students and exchange visitors and shares data with 
other federal agencies with national security, immigration enforcement, 
and immigration benefit adjudication responsibilities.\12\ This allows 
government agencies to access essential information to preserve 
national security and monitor immigration compliance.\13\ The Secretary 
has authority to determine which aliens, including F-1 nonimmigrant 
students, are ``authorized'' to obtain employment in the United 
States.\14\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \12\ See U.S. Immigration & Customs Enf't, Student and Exchange 
Visitor Program, <a href="https://www.ice.gov/sevis">https://www.ice.gov/sevis</a> (last updated Aug. 11, 
2026).
    \13\ Id.
    \14\ See generally INA 274A(h)(3), 8 U.S.C. 1324a(h)(3).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    As stated, DHS derives its authority to administer the programs in 
which F nonimmigrant students engage, and the institutions that run 
these programs, from several sources.\15\ Additionally, Section 641 of 
the Illegal Immigration Reform and Immigrant Responsibility Act of 1996 
(IIRIRA) \16\ authorized the creation of a program for ongoing 
collection of current information provided by schools and exchange 
visitor programs regarding F and other nonimmigrants during the course 
of their stays in the United States, using electronic reporting 
technology where practicable. Consistent with this statutory authority, 
DHS manages these programs pursuant to Homeland Security Presidential 
Directive-2 (HSPD-2), Combating Terrorism Through Immigration Policies, 
as amended,\17\ and Section 502 of the
[[Page 64568]]
Enhanced Border Security and Visa Entry Reform Act of 2002.\18\ HSPD-2 
requires the Secretary to conduct periodic, ongoing reviews of schools 
certified to accept F nonimmigrant students, and to include checks for 
compliance with recordkeeping and reporting requirements.\19\ This 
includes the ability for DHS to verify through records that an SEVP-
certified institution has filed all appropriate paperwork and fees to 
continue to participate in the SEVP program.\20\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \15\ See Section 102 of the Homeland Security Act of 2002 (Pub. 
L. 107-296, 116 Stat. 2135), 6 U.S.C. 112; INA 103(a)(1), (3), 8 
U.S.C. 1103(a)(1), (3).
    \16\ Illegal Immig. Reform and Immigrant Responsibility Act of 
1996, Public Law 104-208, 110 Stat. 3009-546, 3009-704 (Sept. 30, 
1996) (codified as amended at 8 U.S.C. 1372).
    \17\ Homeland Sec. Presidential Directive-2, Combating Terrorism 
Through Immig. Policies (Oct. 29, 2001), available at <a href="https://georgewbush-whitehouse.archives.gov/news/releases/2001/10/20011030-2.html">https://georgewbush-whitehouse.archives.gov/news/releases/2001/1
    \18\ Enhanced Border Security and Visa Entry Reform Act of 2002, 
Public Law 107-173, 116 Stat. 543, 563 (May 14, 2002) (codified at 8 
U.S.C. 1762).
    \19\ See Homeland Security Presidential Directive-2, supra note 
17.
    \20\ Id.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    As part of DHS's authority to administer the programs F 
nonimmigrant students engage in and regulate the institutions in which 
they enroll, Section 502 of the Enhanced Border Security and Visa Entry 
Reform Act of 2002 directs the Secretary to review the compliance with 
recordkeeping and reporting requirements under INA 101(a)(15), 8 U.S.C. 
1101(a)(15)(F), and Section 641 of IIRIRA, 8 U.S.C. 1372, for all 
schools approved for attendance by F-1 nonimmigrant students within two 
years of enactment, and every two years thereafter. Moreover, as is the 
case with all DHS programs, the programs discussed in this proposed 
rule are carried out in keeping with DHS's primary mission, which 
encompasses the responsibility to ``ensure that the overall economic 
security of the United States is not diminished by the efforts, 
activities, and programs aimed at securing the homeland.'' \21\ DHS's 
authority to regulate SEVP-certified institutions has historically 
authorized regulations applying to educational institutions throughout 
8 CFR 214, including requiring schools to file a petition for 
certification and recertification, as the Secretary has deemed them 
necessary to administer the program.\22\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \21\ See 6 U.S.C. 111(b)(1)(F).
    \22\ E.g. 8 CFR 214.2(f)(6)(iii)(A-C) (authorizing the DSO to 
reduce a student's course load); 214.2(f)(6)(iii)(E) (requiring DSOs 
to update SEVIS); 214.2(f)(7) (establishing the conditions under 
which a DSO is permitted to grant and prohibited from granting an 
extension of stay and requiring DSOs to update SEVIS); 
214.2(f)(9)(ii)(D) (requiring DSO to complete employment 
authorization certification in SEVIS); 214.2(f)(9)(iii) (requiring 
educational institutions to provide SEVP access to certain 
information); 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C) (requiring DSOs to recommend OPT 
extension as a prerequisite for nonimmigrant students 
participation); 214.2(f)(11)(ii) (requiring additional DSO 
responsibilities, such as recommending a nonimmigrant for OPT as a 
condition of nonimmigrant students' participation and maintaining an 
OPT student's SEVIS record); and 214.3 (requiring schools to file a 
petition for certification or recertification with SEVP).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
B. Regulatory History
    Historically, the statutes have largely remained silent on the 
definition of practical training, even after the passage of the INA in 
1952,\23\ leaving executive agencies with regulatory authority to 
define and shape the scope of the practical training programs over the 
years.\24\ In 1991, the Immigration and Naturalization Service (INS) 
articulated that practical training had to be ``directly related to 
[the student's] major area of study,'' \25\ a requirement that had been 
stated more generally in the prior regulations under 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10) 
(Jan. 1, 1991) (requiring the training to be ``related''). The 
requirement that practical training be ``directly related'' to the F-1 
nonimmigrant student's ``major area of study'' remains in the current 
regulations.\26\ Thereafter, in 1992, the former INS reorganized the F-
1 nonimmigrant student regulations and further clarified the scope of 
practical training by establishing Optional Practical Training (OPT). 
Among other things, this rule prescribed that OPT could take place 
while school was in session--provided the OPT did not exceed twenty 
hours a week--and after the program of study was completed.\27\ In 
2008, DHS officially coined two types of OPT as pre-completion and 
post-completion OPT.\28\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \23\ See, e.g., Immig. and Nationality Act of 1952, Public Law 
82-414, 101(a)(15)(F), 402(f), 66 Stat. 163, 168, 276 (June 27, 
1952) (addresses student visa and amends the United States 
Information and Educational Exchange Act of 1948, 22 U.S.C. 1446, 
but it is silent on any practical training experiences for 
students); Immig. Act of 1924, Public Law 68-139, 4(e), 43 Stat. 
153, 155 (May 26, 1924) (establishes student visa category but does 
not address practical training).
    \24\ Since 1947, federal regulations have provided that alien 
students may work in cases where employment for practical training 
is required or recommended by their educational institution and 
approved by immigration officials. See, e.g., 12 FR 5355, 5357 (Aug. 
7, 1947) (``In cases where employment for practical training is 
required or recommended by the school, the district director may 
permit the student to engage in such employment for a six-month 
period subject to extension for not over two additional six-month 
periods . . .''); 18 FR 3526, 3529 (June 19, 1953) (``Whenever 
employment for practical training is required or recommended by the 
institution or place of study attended by the applicant, the 
district director or officer in charge having administrative 
jurisdiction over the place in which the institution is located may 
permit such employment of the alien for a six-month period subject 
to extension for not over two additional six-month periods . . .''); 
38 FR 35425, 35426 (Dec. 28, 1973) (``If a student requests 
permission to accept or continue employment in order to obtain 
practical training, an authorized school official must certify that 
the employment is recommended for that purpose and will provide the 
student with practical training in his field of study . . .''). See 
also Congressional Research Service (CRS), Optional Practical 
Training (OPT) for Foreign Students in the United States (Apr. 9, 
2024), available at <a href="https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/IF12631">https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/IF12631</a>.
    \25\ Nonimmigrant Classes, Students, F and M Classifications, 56 
FR 55608 (Oct. 29, 1991).
    \26\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10) (current).
    \27\ See Pre-Completion Interval Training: F-1 Student Work 
Authorization, 57 FR 31954 (July 20, 1992).
    \28\ See Extending Period of Optional Practical Training by 17 
Months for F-1 Nonimmigrant Students With STEM Degrees and Expanding 
Cap-Gap Relief for All F-1 Students With Pending H-1B Petitions, 73 
FR 18944 (Apr. 8, 2008).

8 CFR Part 214
[DHS Docket No. ICEB-2026-0100]
RIN 1653-AB01
Optional Practical Training Fees
AGENCY: U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE), Department of 
Homeland Security (DHS).
ACTION: Notice of proposed rulemaking (NPRM).
-----------------------------------------------------------------------
SUMMARY: This rule proposes new fees for Optional Practical Training 
(OPT) to combat fraud, strengthen the integrity of the immigration 
system, and protect U.S. workers. The following fees would apply for 
each F-1 nonimmigrant student to engage in OPT through a Student and 
Exchange Visitor Program-certified institution: $70,000 for initial 
OPT; and $30,000 for any subsequent OPT.

be received by December 7, 2026.

D. Practical Training for F-1 Nonimmigrants
    Students in F-1 status are nonimmigrants who enter the United 
States with the primary purpose of pursuing an academic or language 
training course of study at an SEVP-certified school or program.\39\ 
With limited exceptions, an F-1 nonimmigrant student may not be 
employed in the United States.\40\ The regulation on practical training 
under 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10) provides that an F-1 nonimmigrant student who 
has been enrolled in post-secondary study on a full-time basis for at 
least one full academic year at a college, university, conservatory, or 
seminary certified by SEVP, and who has otherwise maintained 
nonimmigrant status, may be authorized practical training.\41\ The 
current regulations provide two types of practical training: Curricular 
Practical Training (CPT) and OPT. The scope of this proposed rule is 
specific to OPT and does not address CPT.\42\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \39\ See INA 101(a)(15)(F), 8 U.S.C. 1101(a)(15)(F)(i) ``an 
alien having a residence in a foreign country which he has no 
intention of abandoning, who is a bona fide student qualified to 
pursue a full course of study and who seeks to enter the United 
States temporarily and solely for the purpose of pursuing such a 
course of study . . . '' See also 8 CFR 214.2(f).
    \40\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(9)-(10); 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(6).
    \41\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10).
    \42\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(i).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. Optional Practical Training
    OPT is practical training that is directly related to the F-1 
nonimmigrant student's major area of study but not necessarily an 
integral part of the established curriculum.\43\ OPT is not mandatory 
to fulfill the requirements of an F-1 nonimmigrant student's program of 
study, and F-1 nonimmigrant students can engage in OPT before (pre-
completion OPT) or after program completion (post-completion OPT). F-1 
nonimmigrant students who have participated in one year or more of 
full-time CPT are ineligible for post-completion OPT, but part-time CPT 
does not trigger this limitation on OPT.\44\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \43\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii).
    \44\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(i).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    To engage in OPT, an F-1 nonimmigrant student must first obtain a 
recommendation from a Designated School Official (DSO) at the relevant 
educational institution, who must ensure that the F-1 nonimmigrant 
student is eligible for the type and period of OPT and aware of the 
responsibilities for maintaining status while engaged in OPT.\45\ For 
the initial 12-month OPT period, an F-1 nonimmigrant student does not 
have to secure a training opportunity before the DSO can recommend 
OPT.\46\ Upon issuing the OPT recommendation, the DSO provides the F-1 
nonimmigrant student with a signed Form I-20, and the educational 
institution must assume the added responsibility of maintaining the 
student's SEVIS record for the entire period of authorized OPT.\47\ The 
F-1 nonimmigrant student will then file a Form I-765, Application for 
Employment Authorization, with USCIS, which will adjudicate the 
application on the basis of the DSO's recommendation and other 
eligibility considerations.\48\ Additionally, if an F-1 nonimmigrant 
student is requesting OPT after the F-1 nonimmigrant student's initial 
4-years of admission, the F-1 nonimmigrant student would also need to 
apply for an extension of stay (EOS) with USCIS in addition to the Form 
I-765. See 8 CFR 214.1(c); 214.2(f)(7)(v). Alternatively, if the F-1 
nonimmigrant student does not wish to apply for an EOS, that F-1 
nonimmigrant student would need to depart the United States, and upon 
return, seek admission to the United States through CBP for a new 
admission period. See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(D).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \45\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(11)(i)-(ii).
    \46\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(11).
    \47\ 8 CFR 214.2(f)(11)(ii).
    \48\ 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii), (f)(11)(iii).
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
2. STEM OPT Extension
    F-1 nonimmigrant students with certain qualifying STEM degrees \49\ 
who
[[Page 64570]]
finish their program of study and participate in an initial period of 
post-completion OPT have the option to apply for a STEM OPT extension 
of 24 months, if the STEM degree is in a field of study included on the 
DHS STEM Designated Degree Program List and from an accredited U.S. 
educational institution.\50\ The F-1 nonimmigrant student must apply 
for employment authorization through a Form I-765, Application for 
Employment Authorization, with USCIS to obtain employment authorization 
for STEM OPT.\51\ Additionally, if an F-1 nonimmigrant student is 
requesting a STEM OPT extension after the F-1 nonimmigrant student's 
initial 4-years of admission, the F-1 nonimmigrant student would also 
need to apply for an extension of stay (EOS) with USCIS in addition to 
the Form I-765. See 8 CFR 214.1(c); 214.2(f)(7)(v). Alternatively, if 
the F-1 nonimmigrant student does not wish to apply for an EOS, that F-
1 nonimmigrant student would need to depart the United States, and upon 
return, seek admission to the United States through CBP for a new 
admission period. See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(D). The STEM OPT 
regulations provide additional requirements for the educational 
institutions, F-1 nonimmigrant students, and their employers, for 
participating in STEM OPT.\52\ A student must submit the Form I-983, 
Training Plan for STEM OPT Students or successor form, which includes 
the employer's attestation of adherence to the training plan, to the 
student's DSO at his or her most recent educational institution, prior 
to the new DSO recommendation for STEM OPT.\53\ For STEM OPT, DHS may 
conduct site visits at the employer locations where the F-1 
nonimmigrant students are employed.\54\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \49\ For purposes of STEM OPT, a STEM field is one included in 
the Department of Education's Classification of Instructional 
Programs taxonomy within the two-digit series containing 
engineering, biological sciences, mathematics, and physical 
sciences, or a related field. 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C)(2). DHS 
periodically publishes the DHS STEM Designated Degree Program List 
which includes a complete list of fields of study that DHS considers 
to be STEM fields of study for purposes of the 24-month STEM OPT 
extension. See U.S. Immig. and Customs Enf't, DHS STEM Designated 
Degree Program List, available at <a href="https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/stemList2024.pdf">https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/stemList2024.pdf</a> (last updated July 22, 2024).
    \50\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C). A list of institutional 
accrediting agencies recognized by the Secretary is available at the 
U.S. Department of Education's website. See U.S. Dep't of Educ., 
Off. of Postsecondary Educ., Institutional Accrediting Agencies, 
available at <a href="https://www.ed.gov/laws-and-policy/higher-education-laws-and-policy/college-accreditation/institutional-accrediting-agencies">https://www.ed.gov/laws-and-policy/higher-
    \51\ 8 CFR 214.2(f)(11)(i)(C). If the F-1 nonimmigrant ``timely 
and properly'' files Form I-765, the student's Employment 
Authorization Document (EAD) is automatically extended until USCIS 
adjudicates the pending Form I-765 or for up to 180 days from the 
date that the prior EAD expired, whichever comes earlier. Id. See 
also 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(6)(iv).
    \52\ Such requirements include the employer's participation in 
E-Verify; reporting requirements by the institutions, students, and 
employers, such as material changes or early termination/departure; 
and the student's self-evaluation reports on progress toward the 
training goals. 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C)(5)-(C)(10); 214.2(f)(11), 
(f)(12).
    \53\ 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C)(7). On Form I-983, the student 
and employer must identify the STEM OPT learning objectives and a 
training plan, and the employer must attest the following: that it 
has sufficient resources to provide such training; that the student 
will not replace U.S. workers; that the terms and conditions of the 
training are commensurate with similarly situated U.S. workers; and 
that the STEM OPT will assist the student in reaching the training 
goals. See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C)(6)-(10).
    \54\ See 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C)(11). During the site visit, 
DHS verifies that the information reported on the F-1 nonimmigrant 
student's Form I-983 about the training opportunity is accurate. 
Site visits are limited to checking information related to the F-1 
nonimmigrant student's STEM OPT employment and ensuring that F-1 
nonimmigrant students and employers are engaged in work-based 
learning experiences that are consistent with the information 
supplied on the F-1 nonimmigrant student's Form I-983. As part of a 
site visit, DHS may confirm that the employer has sufficient 
resources and supervisory personnel to effectively maintain the 
program and ask employers to provide the evidence they used to 
assess wages of similarly situated U.S. workers.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
IV. Need for Rulemaking
A. Combating Fraud and Abuse of OPT
    With the increased number of F-1 nonimmigrant students being 
recommended by DSOs for OPT, SEVP has encountered more institutions, 
DSOs, employers, and F-1 nonimmigrant students engaged in schemes to 
exploit the current OPT regulations for fraudulent purposes. Reports 
indicate that there has been widespread use of OPT as an informal and 
unlimited employment benefit that has little to or no relationship to 
an F-1 nonimmigrant student's program of study.\55\ This has resulted 
in DHS devoting significant resources to identifying and investigating 
cases in which improper OPT authorization has been used to facilitate 
F-1 nonimmigrant student visa fraud.\56\ The egregious cases of fraud 
and abuse of OPT \57\ demonstrate that additional action is needed to 
combat such problems. While the SEVP-certified educational institutions 
are entrusted with being the initial gatekeepers in determining whether 
to recommend an F-1 student for OPT, the sheer numbers of F-1 students 
\58\ and fraudulent cases \59\ in OPT in recent years indicate that the 
gates have opened too widely, permitting the program to be flooded with 
participants seeking to exploit the program for improper purposes.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------

available at <a href="https://www.ice.gov/news/releases/[REDACTED]">https://www.ice.gov/news/releases/[REDACTED]
Enf't, HSI data shows rampant foreign student fraud--ICE director 
says this is only the beginning, YouTube (May 12, 2026), available 
at <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fdnwUGmXkT0">https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fdnwUGmXkT0</a>.

    Over the years, the practical training program has confronted 
egregious cases of abuse, particularly with OPT. For example, in 2020, 
a Chinese businesswoman was convicted for conspiracy to commit visa 
fraud for providing false employment documents to at least 2,693 
aliens, primarily Chinese F-1 nonimmigrant students seeking OPT, in 
exchange for a fee.\60\
[[Page 64571]]
She founded two shell companies through which she advertised fraudulent 
visa-related employment and provided false offer letters and employment 
verification letters.\61\ Her company provided assistance to an F-1 
nonimmigrant student who was convicted of conspiracy to act as an agent 
of a foreign government, acting as an agent of the People's Republic of 
China (PRC), and making a materially false statement to the United 
States Army.\62\ Separately, in 2020, ICE arrested 15 F-1 nonimmigrant 
students who were charged with fraudulently using OPT to remain in the 
United States.\63\ Strikingly, there have been alarming reports of 
shell companies selling jobs to foreign students, bypassing H-1B visa 
caps and fees.\64\ In 2021, a news article reported that it had 
identified more than a dozen potential shell companies, which in the 
aggregate, employed nearly 4,000 F-1 nonimmigrant students through 
OPT.\65\ Furthermore, in 2025, during employer site visits in Northern 
Virginia, ICE personnel discovered evidence of fraud and non-compliance 
associated with OPT, ``including evidence of nonfunctional or staged 
worksites and supervisors who lacked knowledge of laws and regulations 
related to employing foreign students while visiting local IT services 
companies.'' \66\

    Most recently, DHS announced an investigation that identified more 
than 10,000 F-1 nonimmigrant students who were engaged in OPT and were 
working for highly suspect employers.\67\ Key findings included the 
following: nationwide fraud, with problematic worksites across multiple 
states; empty buildings and false claims of employment, including 
``pay-to-stay'' visa fraud with nonexistent worksites; residential 
addresses listed as worksites with no employees present; financial red 
flags, including tax liens and suspicious transactions; missing 
employment records and offshore operations; and ``phantom employees'' 
who never reported for work.\68\ The probe found multiple employers 
claiming the same address as the location of their business, but 
further investigation found that none of the employers was actually 
listed on the lease of the building.\69\ Furthermore, the investigation 
revealed that many employers listed residential addresses as work sites 
for hundreds of foreign students, yet no employees were present.\70\
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    \67\ U.S. Immigr. & Customs Enf't, HSI data shows rampant 
foreign student fraud--ICE director says this is only the beginning, 
YouTube (May 12, 2026), available at <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fdnwUGmXkT0">https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fdnwUGmXkT0</a>.
    \68\ Id. at 1:40-2:59. One employer even claimed over 150 
student employees but only one student was present and was trained 
remotely by a foreign company. Id. at 5:45-6:00.
    \69\ Id. at 2:04-2:13.
    \70\ Id. at 2:13-2:20.
---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    Specifically, HSI site visits at STEM OPT worksites found 
coordinated employer clusters, with identical websites and shared 
personnel, falsely denying business relationships.\71\ There were also 
findings of shell companies with multiple employers established by the 
same owner/operator.\72\ Additionally, HSI found multi-state networks 
farming out students to unreported third-party employers and evidence 
of international financial fraud involving complex transactions.\73\ 
Criminal activities were detected among employers located throughout 
the country, including Texas, Georgia, New Jersey, and New York.\74\

granted Employment Authorization Documents (EADs) for pre-completion or 
post-completion OPT and reported working for an employer in the 2024 
calendar year, compared to 160,627 F-1 nonimmigrant students who

    As of June 2026, there were approximately 6,186 SEVP-certified 
schools (ranging from the K-12 level to universities), divided into 
approximately 60 territories, each of which typically has one SEVP 
field representative.\77\ The SEVP field representative is responsible 
for making site visits to each school in their territory (potentially 
hundreds, depending on the territory) at least once a year where the 
representative will typically review school and nonimmigrant student 
records in SEVIS, provide training, and answer questions about SEVP 
requirements and regulations.\78\ School visits made by a SEVP field 
representative and other DHS site visits to schools, employers, and F-1 
nonimmigrant student employment sites are complex, multilayered, and 
require considerable time, resources, and coordination with other HSI 
components and occasionally other federal agencies depending on the 
scope of the investigation.\79\

efforts to limit or eliminate the practical training programs.\80\
