# F-1 Visa
F-1 is the student status the whole track hangs from: CPT, OPT, STEM OPT, cap-gap, and finally H-1B are either exceptions carved out of it or exits from it. This note holds the rules of the status itself so the work-authorization notes can assume them. Read it first when another note says "maintain status" and you need to know what that costs.
## What The Status Is
==F-1 status is granted for one purpose, a full course of study at a school certified by the Student and Exchange Visitor Program, so every right to work is an exception carved out of that purpose.== The statute describes a bona fide student with a residence abroad who enters "temporarily and solely for the purpose of pursuing" that course of study, as DHS restates it in the [[OPT Fee Proposal|OPT fee notice]]. That is the opposite design from H-1B, where the status exists because of a job and ends when the job does ([[H1B Visa]]).
DHS leans on this purpose in its current rulemaking. The OPT fee notice says the F-1 visa "was never intended to provide a pathway to employment in the United States." That sentence is the government's position, not settled law, but it explains why each 2026 change tightens the employment exceptions rather than the study itself ([[Current News]]).
## The Documents That Define Status
1. **Form I-20**
	The "Certificate of Eligibility for Nonimmigrant Student Status," issued by a [[SEVP and DSOs|designated school official]]. The DSO certifies under penalty of perjury that it is true and correct, which is why DSOs treat every endorsement as personal liability ([[CPT Broadcast Messages]]). Its employment page carries the CPT and OPT endorsements, and a student may begin CPT only after receiving an I-20 with the DSO's endorsement.
2. **SEVIS record**
	The government database the DSO updates. For OPT, the recommendation must be entered in SEVIS before the student can apply to USCIS, and the student must then receive a new I-20 showing it ([[OPT Program]]).
3. **Form I-94**
	The arrival and departure record. Today it shows "D/S" (duration of status). Under the postponed fixed-period rule it would show an Admit Until Date instead ([[Duration of Status Rule]]).
4. **Employment Authorization Document (Form I-766)**
	The work permit USCIS issues for OPT, STEM OPT, and the rare hardship category. CPT does not use one: the August broadcast says students "are not expected to obtain EADs for this training" ([[EAD and OPT Processing]]).
## Maintaining Status
==Status survives on three habits: a full course of study every term, normal progress toward the degree, and no work that a DSO or USCIS has not authorized.==
- *Full course of study:* for an undergraduate at a college or university, at least 12 semester or quarter hours per term. Online or distance classes count for no more than one class or three credits per term.
- *Reduced load:* only with the DSO's approval before the student drops, and only for listed reasons. Initial academic difficulty (once per program level), a documented medical condition (up to 12 months in total), or the final term when fewer courses are needed. Dropping below full-time without approval puts the student out of status.
- *Vacations:* the annual or summer vacation counts as in status if the student is eligible and intends to register for the next term. This is why summer work is a legal question and not a status question ([[F-1 Employment Options]]).
- *Normal progress:* a program that will not finish by the I-20 end date needs a program extension requested before that date, for a compelling academic reason, documented illness, or circumstances beyond the student's control.
- *Reinstatement:* USCIS may consider reinstating a student on Form I-539 with a DSO recommendation. It is a discretionary last resort, not a right.
- *On-campus assistantships:* work under a scholarship, fellowship, or assistantship counts as part of the academic program for the full-course-of-study test.
## Time Limits And Grace Periods
The rules in force today are the duration of status rules. A student may enter up to 30 days before the program start date and stay while pursuing a full course of study or authorized practical training. After studies and any practical training end there is a 60-day period to prepare for departure or transfer, and 15 days if the DSO authorized a withdrawal. A student who fails to maintain status gets no departure period.
The fixed-period rule would replace this with an Admit Until Date of up to four years plus 30 days to arrive and 30 days to depart. A federal court postponed it on September 14, so the 60-day period is the operative one for now ([[Duration of Status Rule]]). Dates in this note and in [[2028 Graduation Timeline]] use the 60-day rules and flag where the 30-day version would change them.
> [!WARNING]
> The "current" eCFR view of 8 CFR 214.2 already prints the postponed 30-day wording. Use the version dated 2026-07-01 for the rules actually in force.

## Who Decides What
| Decision | Decided by | Paper trail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| I-20 issue and endorsement, CPT authorization, OPT recommendation, program extension, reduced course load | The school's DSO ([[SEVP and DSOs]]) | I-20 and SEVIS |
| OPT and STEM OPT work permit, hardship work permit, reinstatement, extension of stay under the postponed rule | USCIS | Forms I-765 and I-539 |
| Admission, Admit Until Date, and whether a returning student is admitted | CBP at the port of entry | Form I-94 |
| School certification, broadcast messages, and what counts as a compliant DSO decision | SEVP, a program inside ICE | Broadcast messages |
## Where I Stand
I am at the University of Minnesota Twin Cities, a junior in Fall 2026, with a computer science degree. The internship research notes already filter for a Spring 2028 graduation ([[Companies giving OPT & CPT]]), so every window here counts from a program end date in spring 2028. The exact date on the I-20 decides all of them, so confirm it with ISSS before trusting a calendar.
Practical training requires one full academic year of full-time enrollment, which a junior has met on its face. Computer Science (CIP 11.0701) is on the DHS STEM Designated Degree Program List, but the code printed on the I-20 is what counts ([[STEM OPT Extension]]). The University of Minnesota is also a member of the Association of American Universities, one of the plaintiffs challenging the CPT guidance ([[CPT Lawsuit]]).
## Failure Modes
- Working off campus without authorization, including unpaid or benefit-in-kind work. ISSS lists a free-rent caretaking job as employment that needs authorization.
- Dropping below full-time without the DSO's approval first.
- Leaving the country during the 60-day period before filing for OPT. ISSS warns this ends the rest of the period and the eligibility to apply.
- Trusting a university page without its date. Several pages still say 60 days, others already say 30, and the court order sits between them.
- Treating a lawsuit filing as a rule change. A complaint changes nothing until a court rules ([[Visa Litigation Tracker]]).
## Open Questions
- [ ] Confirm the exact program end date and the CIP code on my I-20 with ISSS 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] Ask ISSS how it treats a student who must leave and re-enter if the fixed-period rule takes effect mid-degree
## Sources
- [8 CFR 214.2(f), version dated July 1, 2026](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2): admission, status, full course of study, employment.
- [Federal Register, OPT fee proposal, 91 FR 64566](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees): DHS's description of the F-1 purpose.
- [DHS Study in the States, duration of status FAQ](https://studyinthestates.dhs.gov/elimination-of-duration-of-status-frequently-asked-questions): I-94 and Admit Until Date.
- [ISSS off-campus employment page](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/off-campus) and [post-completion OPT page](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/opt/basics): UMN's own warnings, dated September 2026 and due for review.
- [DHS 2024 STEM Designated Degree Program List](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/stemList2024.pdf): CIP 11.0701, last updated July 22, 2024.

# F-1 Employment Options
An F-1 student may not work in the United States except through a short list of exceptions, and each one has its own authorizer, hours, and failure mode. This note is the full list, so a decision like [[Junior Year Internship Decision]] can compare options on one page. Read it before accepting any offer, paid or not, that starts before graduation.
## The Options Compared
| Option | Who authorizes | Where and when | Hours | Status on 2026-10-23 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| On-campus employment | Incident to status, no application | School premises or an educationally affiliated site | 20 a week in session, full-time in breaks | Open |
| Curricular practical training | The school's DSO | Before graduation, one employer and period per authorization | Part-time or full-time as authorized | UMN paused all applications; only required experiences considered |
| Pre-completion OPT | USCIS after a DSO recommendation | Before graduation, any related job | 20 in session, full-time in breaks | Open, with costs |
| Post-completion OPT | USCIS after a DSO recommendation | After the program ends | Part-time or full-time | Open |
| STEM OPT extension | USCIS after a DSO recommendation | After 12 months of post-completion OPT | At least 20 a week | Open |
| Severe economic hardship | USCIS after a DSO recommendation | Off campus, after one academic year | 20 in session, full-time in breaks | Open, rare |
| Internship with an international organization | USCIS after a DSO certification | Recognized international organizations only | As authorized | Open, rare |
## On-Campus Employment
==On-campus work needs no application, but it must sit on the school's premises or at an educationally affiliated site, and it caps at 20 hours a week while classes are in session.== The regulation allows on-location commercial firms that serve students, such as the bookstore or cafeteria. It excludes a firm like a construction company building a school building, which provides no direct student services.
Work at an off-campus location counts only when the educational affiliation is associated with the school's established curriculum or related to contractually funded research projects at the post-graduate level. The post-graduate wording is easy to miss. It means an undergraduate cannot assume a company that funds a university lab project qualifies. The employment must also not displace United States residents, and the student may work full-time when school is not in session or during the annual vacation.
ISSS adds that students cannot hold federal work-study positions, that on-campus eligibility ends the day the degree requirements are done, and that a student who applied for OPT but has not yet received it cannot keep working on campus unless the job is related to their field of study. This is the only option that works the summer after junior year without a USCIS wait, and it is the one least affected by the 2026 changes ([[Junior Year Internship Decision]]).
## Curricular Practical Training
CPT is authorized by the DSO, not USCIS, and it is employer-specific and date-specific. The 2026 dispute is about what makes a training "integral to an established curriculum." Full detail lives in [[CPT Program]], the [[CPT Broadcast Messages]], and the [[CPT Lawsuit]]. The short version is that SEVP now says only training required of every student in the degree qualifies, and UMN has paused every application while it reviews that guidance.
## Optional Practical Training
OPT is the USCIS-issued work permit for training directly related to the major. A DSO recommends it, the student files Form I-765, and employment cannot start before the approved start date on the card ([[EAD and OPT Processing]]). It comes in three forms. [[Pre-Completion OPT]] is used during the degree, [[OPT Program|post-completion OPT]] after it, and the [[STEM OPT Extension]] adds 24 months for eligible STEM degrees. All OPT is capped at 12 months per education level, and time used before graduation is deducted.
## Hardship And International Organizations
Off-campus work for severe economic hardship requires an unforeseen financial change outside the student's control, a full academic year in status, good standing, and proof that on-campus work is unavailable or insufficient. The DSO recommends it in one-year intervals and USCIS decides. ISSS says the fee applies and USCIS will likely take 4 to 7 months, so it is not a route to a planned internship.
An offer from a recognized international organization under the International Organizations Immunities Act has its own USCIS application path. That is a narrow case and not relevant to a typical software internship.
## What Is Not Authorized
- Any off-campus job that is not CPT, OPT, hardship, or an international organization, whether paid, stipended, or compensated in kind. ISSS lists paid internships, summer and break work, stipends or bonuses, and free housing for caretaking.
- Working before the authorization is approved and the start date on it has arrived. The I-20 endorsement (CPT) or the EAD start date (OPT) is the gate, not the offer letter.
- Working more than 20 hours a week during the term on campus or on pre-completion OPT.
- Remote work from abroad for a foreign employer. No source I read addresses it, so treat it as unresolved until ISSS answers in writing ([[Junior Year Internship Decision]]).
## UMN Snapshot
ISSS says international students "must always obtain work authorization to work off-campus," that off-campus work must relate to the UMN major, and that it encourages advising early in the job search. The ISSS pages were last updated between September 2026 and, for on-campus work, February 2025, and the CPT text says it "may be outdated and will be updated soon." The UMN rewrite is queued, so this snapshot is a reference point and not a current ruling.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS whether a non-credit, non-required summer project with a faculty member at UMN counts as on-campus employment 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] Ask ISSS whether remote work done entirely outside the U.S. for a foreign or U.S. employer needs any authorization
## Sources
- [8 CFR 214.2(f)(9) and (f)(10), version dated July 1, 2026](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2): on-campus, off-campus, hardship, practical training text.
- [ISSS on-campus employment](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/on-campus), [off-campus employment](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/off-campus): UMN's reading of the same rules.
- [USCIS, Optional Practical Training for F-1 Students](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/students-and-exchange-visitors/[REDACTED]): OPT types and deduction rule.

# SEVP and DSOs
Most F-1 rules are written in a regulation, but they are applied by two kinds of people: federal staff in the Student and Exchange Visitor Program (SEVP) and the school officials it certifies. This note explains who they are, what each can do to a student and to a school, and why a policy change reaches me through my own school's advisors before it reaches me through the law. Read it when a decision turns on whether ISSS "will approve" something.
## The Two Layers
==SEVP certifies schools and the schools' designated officials certify students, so a federal policy reaches a student as a school's decision about what it dares to sign.== SEVP is the ICE program office that administers the F-1 program for DHS. It certifies each school to enroll F-1 students under 8 CFR 214.3, tracks students in the SEVIS database, and publishes guidance as broadcast messages and on its public site, Study in the States.
A **designated school official** (DSO) is a regular school employee whom the school nominates and the government approves. A school must name a principal DSO, and it may name more. The CPT complaint lists what a DSO does: issues the Form I-20, maintains each student's SEVIS record, and advises on status, employment, and practical training. At UMN that office is International Student and Scholar Services (ISSS), and students make requests through the MyISSS portal.
## What A DSO Signs
Every I-20 is certified under penalty of perjury as true and correct. Authorizing CPT means re-signing the I-20 with the training details, and recommending OPT means entering the recommendation in SEVIS and issuing a new I-20 before the student can file with USCIS. When a school petitions for certification or recertification, every DSO attests that they intend to comply with all SEVP regulations.
The August broadcast leans on exactly this: it says a DSO who signs a CPT I-20 is "certifying under penalty of perjury" that the training is required or otherwise integral to the curriculum. That is why the August guidance changed behavior within days even though it changed no regulation ([[CPT Broadcast Messages]]).
## What SEVP Can Do To A School
A school that loses SEVP certification immediately loses the ability to enroll new F-1 students, and its current F-1 students would have to transfer, because status depends on studying at a certified school (8 CFR 214.4(i), as the [[CPT Lawsuit|complaint]] describes it). Both August messages name withdrawal of certification as a consequence. Short of that, SEVP field representatives visit each school at least once a year to review records and provide training.
The OPT fee notice gives the scale: about 6,186 SEVP-certified schools as of June 2026, split into roughly 60 territories that each typically have one field representative. DHS says that is too few people to review the number of OPT employers, which is part of its argument for putting a price on OPT instead ([[OPT Fee Proposal]]).
## Broadcast Messages Are Not Regulations
A broadcast message is an email to DSOs, also posted on ICE's SEVIS pages, with a number such as 2608-01. Each carries a disclaimer that it "is not a substitute for applicable legal requirements, nor is it itself a rule or a final action by SEVP" and creates no enforceable right. In 2026 that disclaimer is the legal hinge. The plaintiffs argue the CPT messages are in substance a binding rule issued without notice and comment, while the messages say they only remind DSOs of existing law.
Three 2026 messages matter. BCM 2608-01 and 2608-02 on CPT, and BCM 2610-02, which announces the OPT fee proposal. ICE keeps the official list on its [SEVIS What's New page](https://www.ice.gov/sevis/whats-new), and that list is the first place to check for a new one.
## Reporting Channels In The Messages
BCM 2608-01 tells employers, DSOs, students, and the public to report suspected employment fraud, practical training fraud, or schools or students abusing the regulations to SEVPFraud@hsi.dhs.gov, an address at Homeland Security Investigations. BCM 2608-02 adds a confidential route for DSOs who feel pressured by school leadership to make attestations they believe are non-compliant, through the SEVP Response Center at 703-603-3400 or 1-800-892-4829, or SEVP@ice.dhs.gov, open Monday through Friday, 8 a.m. to 6 p.m. Eastern. Both are in the primary text.
## Why This Layer Is The Binding Constraint
The plaintiffs report that at least one DSO with more than twenty years of experience considered resigning, and that others became reluctant to authorize even training they believed plainly qualified. Yale's office states that since August 24 it processes CPT only when the training is required of all students in the program. UMN paused every application. Those are separate institutional decisions made under the same threat.
The OPT fee proposal shifts the same pressure to a new point. Under it a DSO cannot enter an OPT recommendation in SEVIS until the school has paid, so the school's willingness to pay becomes the gate ([[OPT Fee Proposal]]).
> [!NOTE]
> A court order in the CPT case would bind the government, but each school would still decide how fast to restart authorizations, as the fear of retroactive scrutiny the plaintiffs describe does not vanish on the day of a ruling. Expect ISSS to move after a ruling, not with it.

## Working With ISSS
- Ask in writing, and keep the reply. Verbal assurances do not protect a student when the DSO signs under perjury.
- Request a recommendation through the portal before any application to USCIS. UMN's page says not to submit an OPT application until the new I-20 with the recommendation is in hand ([[OPT Program]]).
- Do not accept a start date before the I-20 endorsement or EAD start date exists ([[F-1 Employment Options]]).
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS who the SEVP field representative for the Twin Cities territory is, and whether any CS program has a required practical training course 📅 2026-10-30
## Sources
- [BCM 2608-01](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm260801.pdf), [BCM 2608-02](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm_260802.pdf), [BCM 2610-02](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm2610-02.pdf): ICE primary texts.
- [CPT complaint, paragraphs 30 to 35](https://www.nafsa.org/sites/default/files/media/document/CPT%20Complaint%20AAU%20v.%20DHS%20.pdf): SEVP certification and DSO duties.
- [Federal Register, OPT fee proposal](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees): school count and field representatives.
- [Yale OISS CPT update](https://oiss.yale.edu/news/cpt-update-sevp-broadcast-messages-and-pending-litigation): an example of a school applying the guidance.

# CPT Broadcast Messages
Two unsigned emails to school officials in August 2026 are the entire reason my school stopped approving CPT. This note captures both in full, section by section, so I never have to rely on someone else's summary of them. Read it before arguing about what the government "announced," and again after any court ruling to see which sentences survived.
## What They Are
==The two messages never admit the rule changed; they restate "integral part of an established curriculum" as "required of all students" and attach perjury and decertification risk to the older reading.== Both are addressed "To: PDSOs and DSOs at SEVP-certified schools." BCM 2608-01 (August 12, 2026) is titled "Reminder of Liability for DSOs Regarding CPT Authorization." BCM 2608-02 (August 24, 2026) is titled "Guidance for Designated School Officials regarding Curricular Practical Training." Each ends with the same disclaimer that it is not "itself a rule or a final action by SEVP" and creates no enforceable right ([[SEVP and DSOs]]).
The regulation they interpret did not change. It still reads that CPT is "an integral part of an established curriculum" and is defined as "alternative work/study, internship, cooperative education or any other type of required internship or practicum that is offered by sponsoring employers through cooperative agreements with the school" ([[CPT Program]]).
## BCM 2608-01, August 12
SEVP says it "has observed a rise in CPT authorizations that do not meet regulatory requirements, specifically those not constituting an integral part of an established curriculum," and is "increasing its scrutiny of CPT approvals."
1. **CPT must be integral to the curriculum**
	DSOs may authorize CPT only when the training is an integral part of an established curriculum and directly related to the major. It "cannot be authorized solely for the purpose of employment or for experiences that are not required or otherwise formally recognized as part of the academic program." SEVP "reserves the right to request documentation and other evidence from DSOs to identify whether CPT is indeed a core and essential part of the student's studies, without which, they would be unable to complete their degree."
	The same paragraph keeps one opening for schools: "SEVP recognizes that the school, not the U.S. government, defines what is and is not required to complete a degree." It then adds that SEVP is "committed to identifying and investigating schools that attempt to undermine or purposely bypass federal regulations or oversight."
2. **Abuse and escalation**
	Students attest under penalty of perjury that they are in the country "solely for the purpose of pursuing a full program of study." If SEVP suspects officials of "abusing CPT to create work opportunities rather than academic studies," they face scrutiny from SEVP and Homeland Security Investigations, "to include potential withdrawal of the school's SEVP certification."
3. **DSO certification and legal responsibility**
	Signing or re-signing the I-20 to authorize CPT certifies under penalty of perjury that the information is true and correct, "including confirming that CPT is required or otherwise integral to the student's curriculum."
4. **Potential liability**
	"Improper CPT authorization, such as for non-integral purposes, may subject both the institution and individual DSO to administrative or legal consequences. This includes withdrawal of SEVP certification and potential penalties for providing false information or misrepresenting the nature of the training."
5. **Actions requested**
	Review all CPT requests and the school's academic requirements "across all fields of study." Authorize CPT "only if its absence would make the attainment of a degree an impossibility and if the work associated with CPT is required for all students pursuing that degree." Keep thorough records of the curricular necessity of each authorization. Consult the SEVP field representative on specific cases.
The message closes by inviting employers, DSOs, students and the public to report suspected fraud to SEVPFraud@hsi.dhs.gov and gives the SEVP Response Center contacts.
## BCM 2608-02, August 24
This message restates the August 12 message, says SEVP's audits found schools describing CPT "as if it is optional practical training," and warns that "failure to comply with SEVP regulations may result in an institution losing certification to enroll foreign students." It then answers seven questions.
1. **How is CPT defined in regulation?** It quotes the regulatory definition above.
2. **How is CPT different from OPT?** OPT "is not intended to fulfill requirements of a student's program of study and successful program completion does not require participation in OPT; it is optional." CPT "fulfills an integral element of an established curriculum and successful completion is contingent on participation in CPT." It defines "integral" by dictionary as "essential to completeness."
3. **Can CPT be part of an elective course?** No: "In any instance where an elective course is optional, making the CPT optional, the integral and required aspects of CPT would not be met." The training must be required of all participating students "regardless of citizenship."
4. **Must all students in the curriculum participate?** Yes. "All students -- foreign and American -- are required to participate in practical training if they are enrolled in an established curriculum where practical training is integral." SEVP says it found schools that had not required everyone in the same curriculum to participate.
5. **Must students request authorization from the DSO?** Yes, so the school can confirm the curriculum, the relation to the major, and update SEVIS. A DSO who fails to maintain this verification "may face significant SEVP sanctions," citing 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(i), 214.3(l)(2), and 214.4(a)(2).
6. **Must all CPT use employers with cooperative agreements?** Yes, with "legitimate, bona fide employers," and "Students are not expected to obtain EADs for this training."
7. **What if a DSO is pressured by school leadership?** A new confidential SEVP tipline takes those reports for review by SEVP and Homeland Security Investigations.
## What The Language Does And Does Not Say
- *It does say:* training must be required of every student in the curriculum, an optional elective fails, and the school decides what is required. The last point matters, because it means a school can create a program where the training is required of everyone enrolled. Question 4 speaks of "an established curriculum that the school has determined requires CPT."
- *It does not say* anything about students already authorized, about whether it applies to past authorizations, or about the for-credit pathway the plaintiffs say the 1991 rule created ([[CPT Lawsuit]]). Silence is not retroactivity, but the plaintiffs say DHS is already asking about earlier CPT.
- *It contains a tension.* Message 1 says CPT cannot be used for experiences "not required or otherwise formally recognized as part of the academic program," which could cover recognized-but-elective training. Its action item then says only "if its absence would make the attainment of a degree an impossibility." Message 2 resolves the tension in the stricter direction.
## How Schools Reacted
Sources here are the plaintiffs' own filings, so treat them as claims until a court weighs them. The complaint says these schools narrowed CPT: Boston University, Clark, Columbia, Cornell, Dartmouth, Foothill-De Anza, Grinnell, Michigan State, Mount Holyoke, Illinois Urbana-Champaign, Penn, Suffolk, Tufts, Wisconsin, Vanderbilt, and Washington University in St. Louis.
The supporting declarations report that Columbia denied Fall CPT to more than 120 international students, Grinnell had at least 13 students lose Fall 2026 internships in finance, consulting and information technology, and Cornell said many programs were considering making experiences mandatory for everyone. Yale's international office says it has processed CPT only for training required of all students since August 24. UMN paused all applications on September 3 ([[CPT Program]]).
## Reading These As A Student
- The messages are addressed to DSOs, so a student cannot "violate" one. The consequence reaches a student as a denied or withdrawn authorization.
- Keep the course syllabus, credit hours, and the original I-20 for any past CPT. The plaintiffs allege DHS officers have started asking about earlier CPT when students apply for other benefits.
- The path that survives the messages is a curriculum where everyone does the training. If a CS program ever adopts one, CPT returns for that program only.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS whether any UMN CS or CSE program has a required practical training course for all enrolled students 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] After Oct 28, read the government's court response to see how it defends the "required of all students" reading
## Sources
- [BCM 2608-01, ICE](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm260801.pdf) and [BCM 2608-02, ICE](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/bcm_260802.pdf): primary text, read from the PDFs.
- [Plaintiffs' memorandum](https://www.aau.edu/uploads/files/general/Memo-in-Support-of-PI-and-Stay-AAU-v-DHS.pdf): declarations cited for school reactions.
- [Yale OISS update](https://oiss.yale.edu/news/cpt-update-sevp-broadcast-messages-and-pending-litigation): one school's practice.

# CPT Lawsuit
Four higher-education groups sued the federal government on October 5, 2026 over the August CPT messages, and the first court date is November 6. This note records the case itself: who is suing, what each count argues, the 35-year history the plaintiffs rely on, and what the docket shows so far. Read it before the hearing, again after the government's response, and once more after any order.
## The Case At A Glance
| Item | Detail |
| --- | --- |
| Caption | Association of American Universities et al. v. U.S. Department of Homeland Security et al. |
| Court and number | U.S. District Court, District of Massachusetts, No. 1:26-cv-14520 |
| Judge | Julia E. Kobick |
| Filed | October 5, 2026 |
| Plaintiffs | AAU, Presidents' Alliance on Higher Education and Immigration, AICUM, NAFSA |
| Defendants | DHS, ICE, DOJ, and their heads in official capacity |
| Plaintiffs' counsel | Jenner & Block LLP |
| Hearing | November 6, 2026, 3:00 PM, Courtroom 3, on the motion for a preliminary injunction and stay |
| Government response due | October 28, 2026 (extended on October 19) |
| Ruling so far | None. No temporary restraining order, no stay |
## The Parties
==The plaintiffs are associations suing for their members, and the University of Minnesota, Twin Cities is on AAU's published member list, so a ruling covering AAU's members would plausibly reach my school.== The AAU describes itself in the complaint as 71 research universities in the United States and Canada, 69 of them American. The Presidents' Alliance represents close to 600 colleges in forty-two states, the District of Columbia, and Puerto Rico. AICUM represents 55 private colleges in Massachusetts. NAFSA has more than 9,000 members at more than 2,300 institutions, including the DSOs themselves ([[SEVP and DSOs]]).
The defendants include the Department of Justice and the Attorney General because the messages warn of penalties for "providing false information," and perjury is prosecuted by DOJ. The scope of any order is not decided: the plaintiffs ask for relief as to themselves, their members, and the members' employees and students, so whether students at non-member schools benefit depends on what the judge grants.
## The Four Counts
Every count arises under the Administrative Procedure Act, which lets a court set aside agency action that is arbitrary, contrary to law, or issued without required procedure.
1. **Arbitrary and capricious**
	An agency changing position must show it knows it is changing, explain why, and weigh the reliance of people who built plans on the old position. The plaintiffs say the messages "do not even acknowledge" they announce a new reading, call themselves "reminders," and ignore institutions' reliance, the cost to students, the lack of a workable alternative, and the educational reasons programs keep training optional.
2. **Unlawfully retroactive**
	Neither message says it applies only going forward, and both threaten consequences for approvals already made. The plaintiffs allege DHS officers have begun asking students about earlier CPT when they apply for other benefits, which would treat past, lawful approvals under the new test.
3. **Contrary to law**
	An agency is bound by its own regulation and cannot amend it through guidance. The plaintiffs say the regulation's text, structure and history conflict with the messages.
4. **Procedurally invalid**
	The messages set binding eligibility standards backed by enforcement threats, which makes them legislative rules that needed notice and comment. DHS published no proposal, took no comments, and claimed no good-cause exception.
==The case turns on one reading: whether "integral part of an established curriculum" means training that earns academic credit, or only training required of everyone.==
## The History Behind The Plaintiffs' Reading
The complaint's regulatory history is the most useful part for understanding why "this was always the rule" is contested.
1. **1947 and 1953**
	The first practical-training regulations allowed work "required or recommended by the school," so training was never limited to degree requirements.
2. **1964 and 1978**
	Students at schools with alternate work-study courses as part of the regular curriculum could take them without a separate work application, with the employment counted as practical training.
3. **1987**
	The term "curricular practical training" first appears. Guidance said an "elective internship that is not required for the attainment of the degree or diploma is considered curricular practical training."
4. **1991**
	INS proposed a rule defining integral training as training "for which students are awarded academic credits." Commenters from hotel management, nursing, law, engineering and teaching asked for required non-credit internships to count too, and the final rule added "required internship or practicum." The final rule also said a student could do elective curricular training "after having been in status for nine months." That is the two-pathway reading: credit, or required if no credit.
5. **1992**
	INS cables said for-credit training "may be mandatory or elective" and that non-credit training must be required.
6. **2013 to 2022**
	An October 2013 SEVP newsletter said CPT "must be part of a student's program of study, either required for completion or optional, but for academic credit." SEVP's DSO training, available from at least 2014 to 2022, said it must be required or the student must receive credit.
7. **March 2025**
	Study in the States still described CPT as training that must be an integral part of the academic program and directly related to the major.
The plaintiffs add a textual argument. The same 1991 rule lets on-campus work happen at an educationally affiliated off-campus location if it is "an integral part of the student's educational program," meant to cover graduate research assistants on professors' grants, which no degree universally requires. "Integral" therefore cannot mean mandatory.
## The Harm Argument
The memorandum rests irreparable harm on four points: lost, time-limited training that cannot be made up (especially for students in their final year), the threat of losing SEVP certification, compliance costs already being spent, and damage to institutions' standing with prospective students. More than 130,000 students were authorized for CPT in 2024, according to ICE's own report that the complaint cites.
The complaint also answers the obvious fallback of pre-completion OPT. It is not a substitute because the student needs school approval plus a USCIS permit, cannot apply until 90 days before the start, carries a full course load, is capped at 20 hours in term, and burns OPT time. As of September 28, USCIS's own case-processing page showed only 80 percent of F-1 student OPT applications finished within five months, the complaint says ([[Pre-Completion OPT]]).
## What The Docket Shows
1. **October 5** The complaint and a motion for a preliminary injunction and stay of agency action were filed. Motions for leave to file an oversize memorandum and for out-of-state lawyers were granted in the days after.
2. **October 13** A notice set the hearing for November 6 at 3:00 PM in Courtroom 3.
3. **October 19** The government appeared and moved to extend its response deadline to October 28, which the judge granted. The motion's original deadline had been October 19.
4. **October 23** Routine notices of appearance and withdrawal. No substantive order.
## What Each Outcome Would Mean
This section is analysis, not source text.
- *Stay or injunction granted:* the August messages are postponed or barred for the covered plaintiffs and their members. A school like UMN could return to the credit-or-required reading, but ISSS would still need to rebuild its process. The government can appeal, and in the duration of status case it filed its appeal 16 days after losing ([[Duration of Status Rule]]).
- *Denied:* the guidance stays in force while the case continues, and schools remain where they are.
- *Partial:* relief limited to plaintiffs' members would leave students elsewhere unprotected.
- *No decision soon:* the docket shows no deadline for a ruling, so planning for summer 2027 on a hoped-for win is a gamble ([[Junior Year Internship Decision]]).
## Open Questions
- [ ] Read the government's October 28 response when it posts to see its defense of "required of all students"
- [ ] Check the docket after November 6 for an order, a bench ruling, or a supplemental briefing schedule
- [ ] Confirm whether UMN is a member of the Presidents' Alliance, which would add a second route to coverage
## Sources
- [CourtListener docket](https://www.courtlistener.com/docket/74919449/association-of-american-universities-v-us-department-of-homeland/): the live record, a RECAP copy of PACER.
- [Complaint, hosted by NAFSA](https://www.nafsa.org/sites/default/files/media/document/CPT%20Complaint%20AAU%20v.%20DHS%20.pdf) and [memorandum in support, hosted by AAU](https://www.aau.edu/uploads/files/general/Memo-in-Support-of-PI-and-Stay-AAU-v-DHS.pdf): the party filings.
- [NAFSA litigation page](https://www.nafsa.org/legal-defense-CPT): plaintiffs' summary, last updated October 8.
- [AAU member list](https://www.aau.edu/about/member-universities): includes the University of Minnesota, Twin Cities.

# CPT Program
Curricular practical training is the work permission that used to make a summer internship simple for an F-1 student, and in 2026 it is the one most disrupted. This note holds how CPT works, what changed in August, what UMN's pages say as of September, and what is realistically left for a computer science major. Read it before planning any internship that starts before graduation.
## What CPT Is
==CPT is training the school treats as part of the degree, which the school's DSO authorizes on the I-20 for one employer and one period, with no USCIS application and no EAD.== The regulation limits it to "an integral part of an established curriculum" and defines it as "alternative work/study, internship, cooperative education or any other type of required internship or practicum that is offered by sponsoring employers through cooperative agreements with the school" (8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(i)).
The mechanics follow from that. The student requests it from the DSO, may begin only after receiving an I-20 with the DSO's endorsement, and the DSO records in SEVIS whether it is full-time or part-time, the employer and location, and the start and end dates. Authorization is for a specific employer and period, so a new employer needs a new I-20 ([[SEVP and DSOs]]).
- *Eligibility:* one full academic year of full-time enrollment, with exceptions for graduate programs that require immediate training.
- *Against OPT:* CPT is school-authorized and tied to the curriculum, OPT is USCIS-authorized and need not be part of any course ([[OPT Program]]).
- *The cost that matters later:* one year or more of full-time CPT makes a student ineligible for post-completion OPT, but part-time CPT does not. ISSS adds that CPT time is generally not subtracted from the 12 months of OPT.
## Two Readings Of "Integral"
Until August 2026 the working reading was two pathways: training that earns academic credit qualifies even if it is elective, and non-credit training qualifies if it is required for the degree. Since August SEVP says only training required of every student in the curriculum qualifies. The reasons each side gives, the 1991 rulemaking history, and the pending challenge are in [[CPT Lawsuit]], and the exact words of the new reading are in [[CPT Broadcast Messages]].
The difference decides my case. A CS student taking an optional internship course for credit fit the old reading and fails the new one.
## Where CPT Stands On 2026-10-23
- The August 12 and 24 messages are in force. No court has stayed them.
- Plaintiffs including the AAU, of which the University of Minnesota is a member, are asking for a stay and injunction. The hearing is November 6 and the government's response is due October 28.
- Many schools have narrowed or paused CPT. The plaintiffs' filings name sixteen, and UMN is one that has paused every application.
- The regulation itself is unchanged, and so is the CPT definition that the OPT fee proposal repeats. That proposal covers OPT only and says it "does not address CPT" ([[OPT Fee Proposal]]).
## UMN Snapshot
This is the state of UMN's pages in September 2026 and is queued for a rewrite.
1. **The pause**
	ISSS says DHS's SEVP "has recently issued updated guidance," that it "may impact CPT eligibility," and that "ISSS is pausing CPT applications." A student with an experience "required for their degree" should contact ISSS for instructions "if applicable." The pages say the content "may be outdated and will be updated soon," and were last updated September 3.
2. **The usual process**
	The CPT must be on the I-20 before work starts. A student first completes the ISSS CPT online workshop and then applies through MyISSS. ISSS cannot change, void, or restore CPT once authorized.
3. **Three categories on the special situations page**
	Required internship, co-op, and "Elective Internship/Employment." The last needs the instructor's authorization form. It is the category the August guidance removes. Extensions of up to 30 days need a letter, and longer ones need a new request.
4. **The CSE co-op route**
	The College of Science and Engineering co-op runs CPT through a co-op course, CSE 4896, which the student must be registered for before ISSS approves. The CSE page quotes about 10 business days at ISSS plus 2 to 5 at the co-op office, carries the same pause banner, and says to start the application only after accepting an offer.
## What Is Left For A CS Major
I understand my degree has no required internship, which I have not yet verified with the department. If that holds, no CPT is available to me today. It would return in three ways: a court order that restores the credit pathway, a UMN curriculum change that makes a practical-training course mandatory for everyone in a program, or DHS withdrawing the guidance. None is scheduled except the November 6 hearing ([[Junior Year Internship Decision]]).
## Failure Modes
- Starting work on an offer letter before the endorsed I-20 exists.
- Assuming an elective for-credit course still qualifies because it did last year.
- Treating the lawsuit as relief. A filing is not an order ([[Visa Litigation Tracker]]).
- Accumulating a year of full-time CPT without knowing it ends post-completion OPT eligibility.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS and the CS department whether any required practical training exists in my program, and how the pause treats an elective for-credit course 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] Ask ISSS what happens to CPT already on an I-20 started before August
## Sources
- [8 CFR 214.2(f)(10), version dated July 1, 2026](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2).
- [ISSS CPT page](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/cpt) and [special situations](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/cpt/special-situations), both updated September 3, 2026.
- [CSE Co-op, international students](https://cse.umn.edu/coop/international-students): the co-op CPT process.
- [Federal Register, OPT fee proposal](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees): the one-year full-time CPT rule and the statement that it does not address CPT.

```

# OPT Program
Optional Practical Training is the F-1 work permit I will actually spend after graduation, and the bridge to H-1B. The vault uses this note as the hub for everything OPT: the standing rules, the clocks, the 2026 changes, and the traps that cost people months of work authorization. Read it before the semester I request a recommendation, and whenever a job posting says anything about sponsorship.
## What OPT Is
==OPT is a work permit the student already has the right to request, not sponsorship from an employer, and it is spent in 12-month units by the calendar whether or not a job is found.== It is temporary employment directly related to the major. USCIS says an eligible student can receive up to 12 months of OPT before completing studies, after completing them, or split across both, and all pre-completion time is deducted from post-completion time. A student becomes eligible for another 12 months on moving to a higher education level.
That is why the vault's job-screening rule reads "no visa sponsorship" as "no H-1B," not "no OPT." OPT is authorization the student holds, and the screening notes exclude a posting only on an explicit citizenship, clearance, or "OPT not accepted" statement ([[Companies giving OPT & CPT]]).
## The Three Forms
- [[Pre-Completion OPT]] happens during the degree, limited to 20 hours in term and full-time in vacations, and it consumes the same 12 months.
- Post-completion OPT starts after the program ends, at least 20 hours a week, and can be followed by the [[STEM OPT Extension]].
- The STEM extension adds 24 months for an eligible degree with an E-Verify employer and a training plan.
## The Steps For Post-Completion OPT
1. **Request the recommendation**
	The student asks the DSO, who enters the recommendation in SEVIS and issues a new I-20. UMN says to request it in MyISSS 100 days before the expected completion and to finish the ISSS OPT workshop first.
2. **File Form I-765 with USCIS**
	Within 30 days of the DSO's SEVIS entry, and between 90 days before and 60 days after the program end date. UMN adds: do not file until the new I-20 is in hand. USCIS warns that photos must be unmounted and unretouched.
3. **Optionally upgrade to premium processing**
	Form I-907 buys a 30-business-day guarantee for OPT applications ([[EAD and OPT Processing]]).
4. **Wait for the card**
	No work is allowed until the approved start date on the EAD. The permit begins on the requested date or the approval date, whichever is later, and the requested date cannot be more than 60 days after the program end date.
5. **Work and report**
	The job must be directly related to the major. The student reports name, address, and employment interruptions to the DSO.
6. **Plan the exit**
	STEM extension, H-1B with cap-gap, or the 60-day period to leave ([[Cap-Gap Extension]]).
## The Clocks
| Clock | Rule | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Total OPT | 12 months per education level | 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10) |
| Outer limit | All initial OPT within 14 months of completing study | 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(A)(3) |
| File with USCIS | 90 days before to 60 days after the program end date | USCIS OPT and I-765 pages |
| After DSO recommendation | File within 30 days | USCIS OPT page |
| Start date | No more than 60 days after the program end date | 8 CFR 214.2(f)(11)(i)(D), version of July 1 |
| Unemployment | 90 days on initial OPT, 150 with STEM | USCIS cap-gap page |
| After OPT ends | 60 days to depart or change status | 8 CFR 214.2(f)(5)(iv) |
| STEM filing | Up to 90 days before OPT ends, within 60 days of the DSO recommendation | USCIS OPT page |
## What 2026 Changed Or Proposes
- A proposed fee of $70,000 for first OPT and $30,000 for each later OPT, charged to the school, with a stated worry from DHS that schools might stop recommending students ([[OPT Fee Proposal]]).
- A final rule that ends automatic extensions for renewal work permits, which does not name OPT but changes the renewal landscape ([[EAD and OPT Processing]]).
- A fixed-period-of-admission rule that would shrink the post-completion windows to 30 days and add an extension-of-stay application, currently postponed by a court ([[Duration of Status Rule]]).
- Slow processing: the CPT complaint cites USCIS data from September 28 showing only 80 percent of F-1 student OPT applications done within five months.
DHS also says the F-1 student payroll tax exemption is part of the problem. F-1 students on practical training are generally not subject to Social Security and Medicare taxes until after their fifth calendar year in F-1 status, which saves an employer 7.65 percent of pay. The OPT fee notice treats that as an incentive to hire F-1 workers over U.S. workers.
## UMN Snapshot
ISSS says OPT lets a student work "either before or after degree completion in a job directly related to their major area of study" and that USCIS authorizes it. It warns that a student who departs the U.S. during the 60-day period before filing "effectively ends" their F-1 status and eligibility to apply. It lets OPT students keep their UMN email until work authorization ends plus 90 days, and asks to be told if a student decides not to use OPT. These pages were updated in September 2026 and are queued for review.
## Failure Modes
- Filing outside the 90-days-before to 60-days-after window, or more than 30 days after the DSO entry.
- Leaving the country in the 60-day period before OPT is approved.
- Letting a slow adjudication run into the 14-month limit, since the lost months do not come back.
- Using all 12 months before graduation and losing the STEM extension ([[Pre-Completion OPT]]).
- Counting unemployment days without checking the 90-day limit.
- Assuming a job offer is authorization.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS how its OPT process changes if the OPT fee rule is finalized before spring 2028 📅 2026-11-30
- [ ] Check USCIS's case processing page myself for the current OPT I-765 time, since it blocked automated access
## Sources
- [USCIS, OPT for F-1 Students](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/students-and-exchange-visitors/[REDACTED]) (updated November 25, 2024) and [Form I-765](https://www.uscis.gov/i-765).
- [8 CFR 214.2(f), version dated July 1, 2026](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2).
- [ISSS post-completion OPT](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/opt/basics) and [pre-completion OPT](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/opt/pre-completion-opt).
- [Federal Register, OPT fee proposal](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees): the payroll-tax discussion and participation figures.

```

The OPT fee notice treats that as an incentive to hire F-1 workers over U.S. workers.

F-1 students on practical training are generally not subject to Social Security and Medicare taxes until after their fifth calendar year in F-1 status, which saves an employer 6.2 percent of pay up to the taxable wage base plus 1.45 percent of total pay. The notice treats that as an incentive to hire F-1 workers over U.S. workers.

# Pre-Completion OPT
Pre-completion OPT is the only off-campus work route still open for a summer internship now that CPT is paused, and it is the one I have decided to avoid before graduation. This note records the full price of using it: lost OPT months, lost STEM eligibility, a larger proposed fee, and an approval clock that can outlast the internship. Read it when an offer arrives that can only start under OPT.
## How It Works
==Pre-completion OPT is ordinary OPT used before the degree is finished, and every month of it is deducted from the post-completion OPT that follows.== A student may apply once they have completed a full academic year of full-time enrollment, and may file Form I-765 up to 90 days before that anniversary or before the requested start date. The student first needs a DSO recommendation and a new I-20. USCIS then adjudicates, and no work starts before the card's approved start date.
- *Hours:* 20 or fewer per week while school is in session. Full-time when school is not in session, such as the summer. UMN says summer may be part-time or full-time.
- *Course load:* the student must still carry a full course of study, because OPT does not replace enrollment.
- *Job:* directly related to the major, but the school does not pre-approve the employer, unlike CPT.
- *Unemployment:* pre-completion OPT has no unemployment limit; post-completion and STEM OPT do.
## What It Costs
1. **Months**
	USCIS is precise about the deduction. One year of part-time pre-completion OPT (20 hours a week) reduces the full-time OPT still available by six months, half the authorized year. One year of full-time pre-completion OPT reduces it by the whole year, and a student in that position "would not be entitled to any period of post-completion OPT." So a 12-week full-time summer costs about three months of post-completion OPT, and a part-time term costs half of that. Those figures are my arithmetic from USCIS's rule.
2. **STEM eligibility**
	ISSS says STEM extensions are not available for pre-completion OPT, that a student must be on post-completion OPT to apply for STEM OPT, and that using all 12 months before graduation loses the opportunity ([[STEM OPT Extension]]).
3. **Money, if the fee is finalized**
	The proposed rule charges $70,000 to the school on the first OPT recommendation of any kind and $30,000 on each later one. DHS spells out that pre-completion followed by post-completion OPT would total $100,000, against $70,000 if all OPT is post-completion, and says it "anticipates that institutions would encourage F-1 nonimmigrants to use only post-completion OPT." Add a STEM extension and the proposed rule's own per-recommendation arithmetic gives $130,000 (my sum, not the notice's). The fee has not been adopted and would apply to recommendations dated after its effective date ([[OPT Fee Proposal]]).
4. **Time**
	The plaintiffs in the CPT case say pre-completion OPT is a poor substitute because a student cannot apply until 90 days before the start and USCIS data showed only 80 percent of F-1 student OPT applications finished within five months as of September 28. Premium processing offers a 30-business-day guarantee ([[EAD and OPT Processing]]).
## The Summer 2027 Timeline
This is analysis built from the rules above. For a mid-June start, the earliest filing is about mid-March 2027, and the DSO recommendation must exist first (UMN's page says to request it in MyISSS 100 days before the start, about early March). Without premium processing, a five-month wait runs past the end of the summer. With it, 30 business days is about six weeks, which fits only if nothing delays the file or triggers a request for evidence. An offer that depends on this route has a real chance of arriving after its own start date.
## Where It Still Makes Sense
- A strong offer that cannot be done any other way, where the part-time deduction is small.
- A situation in which the fee proposal is withdrawn or scaled back, and the STEM extension is not at risk because enough months remain.
- A case where a school-recommended training slot becomes available only for OPT.
## Failure Modes
- Using all 12 months before graduation, which forfeits STEM OPT.
- Counting on the card in time and committing to the start date before it arrives.
- Assuming "20 hours a week" applies in the summer. It does not; vacation work may be full-time, which deducts faster.
- Forgetting that the approved OPT is deducted even if no internship materializes ([[Junior Year Internship Decision]]).
## Open Questions
- [ ] Ask ISSS whether pre-completion OPT is open now, and whether the school has said it will pay the fee if finalized 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] Ask ISSS for its recent turnaround on pre-completion recommendations
## Sources
- [USCIS, OPT for F-1 Students](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/students-and-exchange-visitors/[REDACTED]): the deduction rule.
- [ISSS pre-completion OPT](https://isss.umn.edu/fstudents/employment/opt/pre-completion-opt), updated September 18, 2026: eligibility, hours, STEM, the 100-day recommendation window.
- [Federal Register, OPT fee proposal](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees): the $100,000 versus $70,000 comparison.
- [CPT complaint](https://www.nafsa.org/sites/default/files/media/document/CPT%20Complaint%20AAU%20v.%20DHS%20.pdf): why pre-completion OPT is not a substitute.

# STEM OPT Extension
The STEM extension turns 12 months of post-graduation work authorization into 36, which is what buys three tries at the H-1B lottery instead of one. It is also the part of OPT the government is watching most closely, because it has grown far beyond what DHS forecast. Read this before choosing a first employer after graduation, since that employer shapes eligibility for the next 24 months.
## What It Is
==The STEM extension is 24 more months of work authorization for a graduate whose degree is on the DHS STEM list, available only to someone already on post-completion OPT with an employer that uses E-Verify and signs a training plan.== The regulation caps a student at two lifetime STEM extensions, and a second one must rest on a higher-level degree. The extension begins the day after the initial OPT ends and runs 24 months, regardless of the date the approval arrives.
## Eligibility
- *The degree:* a bachelor's, master's, or doctorate from an accredited U.S. institution, in a field on the DHS STEM Designated Degree Program List. Computer Science, CIP 11.0701, is on the July 22, 2024 list, as are Computer and Information Sciences, General (11.0101) and Information Technology (11.0103). The list is checked at the moment the DSO recommends the extension, using the degree code in SEVIS, so the code on my I-20 is what counts.
- *The base OPT:* an initial grant of post-completion OPT. Pre-completion OPT does not qualify, and a student cannot apply once a cap-gap extension has ended with a denied petition and the 60-day period has begun.
- *The employer:* enrolled in E-Verify and in good standing, with an employer identification number.
- *A prior degree:* if the extension rests on an earlier degree, it must be from a certified, accredited U.S. school and conferred within the previous 10 years.
## What The Employer Signs
Form I-983 is the training plan. The student and employer identify goals, specific skills to be taught, a performance evaluation process, and methods of oversight and supervision, and the employer attests to three things. It has enough resources and personnel to provide the training at the stated location. The student will not replace a full- or part-time U.S. worker, temporary or permanent. And the opportunity assists the student in reaching the training goals.
- *Pay and terms:* duties, hours, and compensation must be commensurate with those of the employer's similarly situated U.S. workers in the area.
- *Hours:* at least 20 hours per week.
- *Reporting:* the employer must report a termination or departure to the DSO within five business days.
- *New employer:* the student submits a new Form I-983 within 10 days of starting and needs a new DSO recommendation.
- *Oversight:* DHS may visit any employer, with 48 hours' notice unless a complaint triggered the visit.
This is the answer to the job-specific question: a startup, a staffing firm, or a small company that is not in E-Verify cannot host a STEM extension, however good the job is. UMN's page points to the E-Verify employer search.
## The Application Clocks
1. **Apply early**
	A student files Form I-765 up to 90 days before the current OPT expires, after the DSO recommendation, and within 60 days of the DSO's SEVIS entry.
2. **Stay covered while it is pending**
	A timely and proper filing, with the signed I-983 given to the DSO, extends the old work permit automatically for up to 180 days or until USCIS decides, whichever is first. This sits in the F-1 regulations and in USCIS's OPT page, and the October 8 OPT fee notice restates it, so I read the October 14 end of automatic renewals as separate ([[EAD and OPT Processing]]).
3. **Unemployment**
	An additional 60 days on top of the 90 on initial OPT, for 150 in all.
## Why DHS Is Looking Hard At It
The OPT fee notice gives DHS's numbers. DHS projected in 2016 that STEM OPT would involve almost 50,000 students in year one and about 92,000 by year ten, which is 2026. By 2024 it counted 165,524 STEM OPT participants alongside 194,554 on other OPT. DHS calls that nearly double the 85,000 annual H-1B limit and says the extension has become a channel to avoid H-1B caps ([[OPT Fee Proposal]]).
DHS also says employer attestations on the I-983 are not verified unless there is a site visit, and it describes site visits finding clusters of employers with identical websites and shared personnel. It quotes the presidential proclamation's unemployment rates for recent graduates, 6.1 percent for computer science majors and 7.5 percent for computer engineering, and argues OPT labor adds to that pressure. These are the government's claims, and the notice is a proposal.
## What Is Proposed
- A fee of $30,000 on the school for each OPT recommendation after the first, which includes a STEM extension.
- A fixed-period admission rule, now postponed, that would require an extension-of-stay application to take the extension ([[Duration of Status Rule]]).
## What It Means For Me
- Computer science is on the list, so the extension should be available if the I-20 shows 11.0701 or another listed code.
- The first post-graduation employer should be in E-Verify and willing to sign an I-983, or I must plan to change employers with a new I-983 within 10 days of starting.
- The extension supplies the second and third H-1B registrations, which is my inference from the OPT and registration calendars ([[H-1B Lottery]], [[2028 Graduation Timeline]]).
## Failure Modes
- Using pre-completion OPT until the 12 months are gone ([[Pre-Completion OPT]]).
- An employer not in E-Verify.
- Filing after the current OPT ends, or after 60 days from the DSO entry.
- A new job with no new I-983 within 10 days.
- Hosting at a company that the government may view as a shell, and being caught in a site visit.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Confirm the CIP code on my I-20 matches the list 📅 2026-10-30
- [ ] Ask ISSS which of its job-search resources identify E-Verify employers
## Sources
- [8 CFR 214.2(f)(10) and (f)(11), version dated July 1, 2026](https://www.ecfr.gov/on/2026-07-01/title-8/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-214/section-214.2): eligibility, I-983, employer terms, filing clocks.
- [DHS 2024 STEM Designated Degree Program List](https://www.ice.gov/doclib/sevis/pdf/stemList2024.pdf).
- [USCIS, OPT for F-1 Students](https://www.uscis.gov/working-in-the-united-states/students-and-exchange-visitors/[REDACTED]) and [Study in the States STEM OPT hub](https://studyinthestates.dhs.gov/stem-opt-hub).
- [Federal Register, OPT fee proposal](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/08/2026-20660/optional-practical-training-fees): participation numbers and site-visit findings.

# EAD and OPT Processing
An OPT approval is only useful when the work permit card arrives, and the card's timing is the least controlled part of the whole track. This note covers the application form, the card, how long it takes, premium processing, the 14-month rule that turns delay into lost work months, and the October rule that ended automatic extensions. Read it before choosing a filing date.
## The Form And The Card
OPT work authorization is requested on Form I-765, Application for Employment Authorization, and issued as an Employment Authorization Document, Form I-766. USCIS lists the F-1 categories as (c)(3)(A) for pre-completion OPT, (c)(3)(B) for post-completion OPT, and (c)(3)(C) for the 24-month STEM extension. The (c)(3)(B) row repeats the window: file up to 90 days before, but no later than 60 days after, the program end date, and within 30 days of the date the DSO enters the recommendation.
- *Before approval:* USCIS says a student "may not begin your pre- or post-completion OPT until after we approve your Form I-765 and you receive your Employment Authorization Document."
- *After approval:* the card should be produced within two weeks, and USCIS asks for a total of 30 days from approval before an inquiry. Case Status Online shows the delivery tracking number.
- *Start date:* employment authorization begins on the date requested or the date the application is adjudicated, whichever is later, and a student cannot request a start more than 60 days after the program end date.
## Processing Time
==The only fact the track can rely on is that the card arrives after a wait measured in months, not weeks, so the filing date has to be set backwards from the date work must start.== The CPT complaint says that as of September 28, 2026, USCIS's Case Processing Times page showed only 80 percent of F-1 student OPT applications completed within five months. That is a party's citation of a government page. USCIS's tool blocked my own automated fetch, so check the live figure by hand before relying on it.
A law firm writing in 2026 says delays have stretched from weeks into months and in some cases past a year. I could not verify the "past a year" claim, and I give it no weight beyond showing the direction of travel.
## The 14-Month Rule
The regulation requires all initial OPT to be completed within 14 months of completing study, except the 24-month STEM extension. Because employment starts at the later of the requested and approval dates, a late approval does not move the end date, so the 12 months shrink. A law firm's illustration is that a student who files promptly but waits five months can end with only about nine months of OPT, having been unable to work during the wait because a pending initial application does not authorize employment. The firm's conclusion that the card should arrive within about two months of graduation to preserve the full year follows from the regulation's arithmetic ([[OPT Program]]).
## Premium Processing
USCIS has accepted premium processing for pre-completion, post-completion, and STEM OPT applications since April 3, 2023, after a phase-in that began March 6. It is requested on Form I-907, online or on paper, either with the I-765 or later as an upgrade of a pending case.
- *The guarantee:* 30 business days for Form I-765, starting when USCIS receives a properly completed I-907 with the correct fee. The fee is set on Form G-1055, which I did not read.
- *What counts as action:* an approval, a denial, a notice of intent to deny, a request for evidence, or opening a fraud investigation. It guarantees action, not approval.
- *If evidence is requested:* the 30-day clock stops and restarts when the response arrives.
- *If missed:* USCIS refunds the premium fee.
## October 14: Automatic EAD Extensions Ended
The final rule "Removal of Automatic Extensions for Certain Employment Authorization Documents," effective October 14, 2026, finalizes an interim rule published October 30, 2025 without change. Before the interim rule, a timely renewal application could extend an expiring work permit for up to 540 days. A renewal filed on or after October 30, 2025 no longer does, unless the Code of Federal Regulations, another law, or a notice for Temporary Protected Status says so. It does not affect permits extended before that date.
I did not find OPT, STEM OPT, or F-1 named in the rule. The STEM 180-day extension lives in a different provision, 8 CFR 274a.12(b)(6)(iv), which USCIS's OPT page and the October 8 OPT fee notice still describe, so I read the STEM extension as unaffected. That is a reading, not a USCIS statement ([[STEM OPT Extension]]).
## The Fixed-Period Rule's Effect
If the postponed duration of status rule takes effect, a student needing OPT beyond the I-20 end date must apply for an extension of stay on Form I-539 and apply for employment authorization at the same time. A student who files for post-completion OPT or STEM OPT within six months of the rule's effective date is exempt from the extension-of-stay application ([[Duration of Status Rule]]).
## Failure Modes
- Filing at the last day of the window because "60 days after" feels safe, then losing months to the 14-month cap.
- Planning a start date around the card's expected arrival.
- Paying for premium processing and treating the 30 business days as an approval date.
- Citing a blog's processing estimate instead of the live page.
## Open Questions
- [ ] Look up the live USCIS processing time for the OPT I-765 myself and note the date 📅 2026-11-02
- [ ] Read Form G-1055 for the current premium fee for Form I-765
## Sources
- [USCIS, Form I-765](https://www.uscis.gov/i-765): the (c)(3) rows and card timing.
- [USCIS, premium processing](https://www.uscis.gov/forms/all-forms/how-do-i-request-premium-processing): the 30-business-day guarantee and OPT eligibility.
- [Federal Register, removal of automatic EAD extensions, 91 FR 65306](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/14/2026-20955/removal-of-automatic-extensions-for-certain-employment-authorization-documents).
- [Reddy Neumann Brown on the 14-month rule](https://www.rnlawgroup.com/[REDACTED]/): a law firm's reading, checked against the regulation.
- [CPT complaint, footnote 6](https://www.nafsa.org/sites/default/files/media/document/CPT%20Complaint%20AAU%20v.%20DHS%20.pdf): the five-month figure.
